#3005. Steelers owner breaks silence on ‘insubordinate’ Aaron Rodgers — and reveals Mike McCarthy’s fate

Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy has the support of team president and owner Art Rooney after Aaron Rodgers accused his now former coach of sleeping on the job.

Rooney made it clear that McCarthy's job is safe in a statement to the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette on Thursday — and he called out Rodgers for "insubordination" regarding the quarterback blasting McCarthy during Wednesday's installment of "The Pat McAfee Show."

"I just want to say that I have 100% confidence in Mike McCarthy and I expect he'll be our coach this season, next season and beyond," Rooney said. "The big thing, what happened this week, is insubordination can't be tolerated in any organization. When you start getting ultimatums, you can't live with that."

Pittsburgh Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy speaks during a news conference after an NFL football game in Philadelphia, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Chris Szagola

Rooney explained that Rodgers was not being a "good teammate" by publicly bashing McCarthy two days before their Black Friday game against the Broncos.

"There's maybe a time and place after the season if he wanted to kind of unload," Rooney said. "He talks about wanting to be a good teammate, but you don't do that to your teammates a couple days before the game, that's for sure.

"In general, I think, historically, what's in the locker room stays in the locker room. That's the way it's always been. That's the way it should be as far as I'm concerned."

Art Rooney II, owner of the Pittsburgh Steelers, looks on before the game against the New England Patriots at Gillette Stadium on Sept. 21, 2025, in Foxborough, Massachusetts. Getty Images

McCarthy said he benched Rodgers after the former four-time NFL MVP presented an "ultimatum" that was "clearly outside of his job description."

He didn't elaborate on their "private conversations."

According to the Post-Gazette, Rodgers gave McCarthy an ultimatum in a meeting Tuesday night: "Either let me call all the plays or just release me."

Aaron Rodgers' NFL options dwindling after ugly Steelers divorce

Mike McCarthy explains 'ultimatum' behind decision to bench Aaron Rodgers

The troubling response to Aaron Rodgers' bombshell claims against Mike McCarthy

Pat McAfee floored by Aaron Rodgers remarks about 'unhealthy' Mike McCarthy that got him cut

McCarthy told Rodgers he doesn't get to make demands, and then had an impromptu meeting with Rooney and general manager Omar Khan that night, sources told the Post-Gazette — adding that it was decided by McCarthy to make Rodgers inactive for the Broncos game with the intention of releasing him.

The Steelers announced Tuesday night that Rodgers would be inactive against Denver this week, and that Mason Rudolph would replace him.

After that, Rodgers did a surprise TV appearance Wednesday and accused McCarthy of sleeping during team meetings — and he was released shortly after his interview.

Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers and head coach Mike McCarthy chat prior to a game against the Eagles at Lincoln Financial Field on Nov. 22, 2026, in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. Getty Images

He also said McCarthy missed a walk-through Friday and a meeting Saturday morning before the Steelers' 19-16 loss to the Eagles on Sunday — adding that it's allegedly a known behavioral pattern exhibited by McCarthy in the Steelers' building.

During his appearance on "The Pat McAfee Show," Rodgers said he confronted McCarthy about dozing off in meetings as a "concerned friend" because it seemed "unhealthy."

Rooney said Rodgers never expressed concerns about McCarthy's health with him.

"Mike's as healthy as the rest of us," Rooney said. "Health was not an issue. I'm not sure where that part comes."

Rodgers explained that "the nail in the coffin" occurred during Sunday's game in Philadelphia, when McCarthy broke up the halftime huddle by saying, "1-2-3 Packers."

Rodgers also said he found out he was going to be inactive from tight end Pat Freiermuth on a phone call Tuesday night, and that being benched was never a discussion with McCarthy.

Rodgers was released Wednesday shortly after the interview.

During a press conference Wednesday, McCarthy did not confirm or deny sleeping in meetings — but he did concede to the "1–2–3 Packers" moment, describing it as embarrassing.

"A change had to be made, and I certainly agree with the change and the way it was handled."

Rodgers returned to Pittsburgh on a one-year, $22.5 million contract for what he said is his final NFL season.

"I'm disappointed the way it came out," Rooney said when asked if he regrets bringing Rodgers into the fold. "We certainly had hopes that he would be successful here, and I thought at times we certainly got on a roll. We had high expectations.

"It's easy to say, knowing what I know today, what would I do? But we had a good experience last year, and so I kind of wasn't expecting it to go this way this year."

McCarthy coached Rodgers with the Packers from 2006 through December 2018 and they won a Super Bowl by beating the Steelers to cap the 2010 season.

#3004. Etihad’s New Credit Cards Are Primarily for Status Chasers

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. The new cards offer perks like faster paths to status and elevated earning rates when purchasing tickets with partners.

Etihad Guest, the loyalty program of Abu Dhabi-based Etihad Airways, is expanding its U.S. presence by introducing two new credit cards. Most people won’t get much value from these cards, but they can make sense for Etihad frequent flyers who want elite status.

The two cards are the Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card ($99 annual fee) and the Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card ($499 annual fee). Issued by the First National Bank of Omaha, both offer faster pathways to earning and maintaining elite status and the ability to earn extra miles on Etihad purchases.

They also offer extra miles on international transactions (neither has foreign transaction fees) and purchases with Etihad’s partner airlines, which include U.S. carriers like American Airlines and JetBlue Airways.

Here’s what you need to know about the cards.

The Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card comes with a $99 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the card is offering the following welcome offer for new applicants: Earn up to 40,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 10,000 miles after the first purchase and an additional 30,000 miles after spending $3,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: You’ll also get Etihad Silver status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account. Unlike most airlines, that entry-level status is enough to get you into the business class lounge when traveling through Etihad’s hub at Abu Dhabi’s Zayed International Airport.

You can retain that status by spending $20,000 a year on the card, or $3,000 a year on Etihad purchases.

The Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card comes with a $499 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

5 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

The only differences in rewards between the premium and standard cards are increased earning rates for eligible airline purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the premium card offers a higher welcome offer compared to the standard card: Earn up to 60,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 20,000 miles after the first purchase and 40,000 miles after spending $7,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: The premium card offers a faster track to elite status: automatic Silver status upon card approval and Gold status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account.

To maintain Gold status, you’ll need to spend $40,000 a year on the card, or $8,000 on Etihad purchases. Otherwise, you’ll have Silver status.

Wi-Fi benefit: After your first Etihad purchase, you’ll get 3,000 Etihad Guest miles, enough to cover Wi-Fi costs on a long-haul Etihad flight. You’ll then get those miles again after each cardholder anniversary.

Travel benefits: The card offers a statement credit for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck and select travel protections. If you already have a premium travel card, you might already have access to these perks.

The card also includes a Priority Pass lounge access membership. However, an Etihad spokesperson told NerdWallet that it’s the entry-level membership tier, so you’ll need to pay for yourself and guests each time you visit a Priority Pass lounge. Most premium credit cards in the U.S. that offer Priority Pass allow complimentary visits for at least the cardholder.

The Etihad cards make the most sense for Etihad’s frequent flyers who are flying to or connecting through Abu Dhabi several times a year.

That’s because the standard card’s accelerated pathway to Etihad Guest’s Silver status tier can unlock the ability to choose business class lounge access in Abu Dhabi as a status perk.

With the premium card, you get a fast track to Gold. If you select lounge access as a Gold status perk, you can not only bring a guest on the same flight into the lounge, but also access Etihad’s worldwide lounge network. That includes Etihad’s own lounges in New York and Washington, D.C., as well as most partner lounges at other Etihad destinations.

Bear in mind that while Etihad has many partnerships with other airlines, it’s not part of an alliance, so you don’t get lounge benefits when traveling on partners. You’ll need to be flying on an Etihad-operated flight to get into the lounge.

If you primarily fly with other carriers, then Etihad status will have limited upside for you.

However, the Priority Pass lounge membership that comes with the premium card does not have that restriction. You can access Priority Pass lounges while flying on any airline, although you’ll need to pay an additional per-person $35 fee each visit.

The earning rates on the Etihad cards are lackluster for everyday spenders. The bonus categories are for airline purchases with Etihad and its partners, as well as international spending.

That means your day-to-day purchases at home, like groceries, dining out and gas, earn only 1 mile per $1 spent.

Plus, there are more ways to earn Etihad Guest miles than spending on an Etihad credit card. For example, you can transfer points to Etihad Guest from Bilt Rewards, Capital One and Citi ThankYou.

Those programs offer cards that typically have better benefits and bonus categories, and they offer flexibility on how you can use your points and miles. With an Etihad card, you can only earn Etihad Guest miles, which expire after 18 months without qualifying flight activity. (If you’re not traveling on Etihad or its partners at least once every 18 months, then you definitely should not get the cards.)

One of the most interesting perks of the new cards is the bonus category for spending with Etihad partner airlines — 2 miles per $1 for the standard card and 3 miles per $1 for the premium version. Typically, airline credit cards reward customers only for spending on the airline, not partners.

There are better cards available, though. Some offer as many or even more points per $1 on flights purchased directly from airlines.

If you want the card solely to earn more Etihad Guest miles, then the answer for most people is no.

While you can earn more miles for purchases with Etihad and partner airlines, the earning rates aren’t attractive enough to justify the annual fees, especially when compared with existing cards with currencies that transfer to Etihad Guest.

Status chasers could benefit from the fast track to elite status — but you would need to be a fairly frequent Etihad flyer to really take advantage of your elite benefits.

You want a travel credit card that prioritizes what’s important to you. Here are some of the best travel credit cards of 2026:

Flexibility, point transfers and a large bonus: Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card

No annual fee: Wells Fargo Autograph® Card

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card

Bonus travel rewards and high-end perks: Chase Sapphire Reserve®

Luxury perks: American Express Platinum Card®

Business travelers: Ink Business Preferred® Credit Card

#3003. Player Grades: Cavs at Knicks – Another fourth quarter implosion

The Cleveland Cavaliers took another painful loss to the New York Knicks after going off the rails in the fourth quarter.

Grades are based on our usual expectations for each player.

30 points, 4 rebounds, 4 assists, 4 steals, 2 blocks, 2 turnovers

Mitchell was lights out in the first half, storming downhill and busting New York's point-of-attack defense. The Knicks had no answer for him as Mitchell began the game 9-for-10 from inside of two-point range.

His defensive activity was strong, as well, with Mitchell racking up stocks throughout the game.

The fourth quarter was less efficient. Mitchell's drives became erratic, including one play where he was lost in the trees twice in the same possession. There's value in putting pressure on the rim, but Mitchell has to do a better job of moving away from pressure when the time calls for it.

It was hard to choose between Mitchell and Harden for Player of the Game. Mitchell's defense tipped the scales.

28 points, 5 assists, 1 rebound, 4 turnovers

Good things happen when Harden touches the paint. He's looked more spry and youthful in recent games, dancing with his dribble to work into the restricted area and create from there. Whether it was shooting a floater, kicking to the perimeter, or dropping it into Jarrett Allen's lap — Harden put on a clinic on how to drive the ball.

Unfortunately, Harden shot 1-of-8 from downtown and wasn't able to sprint through the finish line as everything went wrong in the fourth quarter.

10 points, 5 rebounds, 1 assist, 2 steals

Allen was able to roam freely on defense while Harden accepted the challenge of guarding Karl-Anthony Towns on the perimeter. This strategy worked throughout the night as Cleveland was able to safely navigate New York's off-ball actions with Allen standing in the paint to clean up any mistakes. It wasn't until the wheels came off in the fourth quarter, when the Knicks were able to sprint in transition and break the mundanity of the half-court, that Allen became nonconsequential.

8 points, 5 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 steals, 2 blocks

It feels like I'm trying to figure out what to expect from Watson. He hasn't been featured enough in the offense for me to know where he fits. Watson only attempted four shots tonight and didn't spend much time with the ball in his hands.

Proctor had a rough shooting night (1-for-6) but was able to bolster the Cavaliers' defense by being aggressive away from the ball. Proctor does a good job of using his body and bumping guys who try to cut around him. The Cavs could use more of that mentality.

Sometimes, Thomas plays basketball like an adrenaline junkie. He bursts around the court and tries to get involved in every defensive play. That's helped him cause a handful of turnovers, like when he jump-scared Josh Hart, forcing him to lose the ball out of bounds in the second half.

This was not a standout game for Hezonja. He only attempted one shot in 10 minutes and finished as a minus-14.

Merrill hasn't had it going this season. He shot 1-of-7 from downtown tonight and is shooting 32% from deep this season.

6 points, 6 rebounds, 4 assists, 4 turnovers

Tyson is in a funk. It's that simple. He hasn't looked as confident or spry as he was last season. The Cavs will have to figure out how to correct this if they want to reach their ceiling this season. Tyson is too important as a potential sixth man for this to continue.

His last shot attempt was swatted into the stands.

Bryant gave the Cavs good minutes tonight by boxing out and cleaning the glass. I'm glad we were able to end this on a positive note.

#3002. What Is a Reconditioning Fee on a Used Car?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Used-car dealers may charge you a reconditioning fee to cover their costs of getting cars ready for sale.

A reconditioning fee is an added dealer charge you might find when buying a used car. It’s intended to cover any costs the dealer incurs to make sure a used car is showroom-ready, from mechanical inspections to detailing.

The reconditioning process varies between dealerships, but typically includes these steps:

A visual exterior inspection. An auto tech does a quick walk-around the vehicle and notes any corrosion, damage or wear.

An interior inspection. The engine and auto interior are examined and tested, and any issues are documented.

A test drive. The car is taken out for a drive to check that it runs well and that the radio, brakes, A/C and other components are all functioning properly.

Service and repair. Any interior or exterior mechanical, functional or cosmetic issues that were found are repaired, including paint, dents, oil changes and major engine work.

Inspection. Once repairs are completed, the car is inspected again to make sure everything looks and functions as it should.

Detailing. This includes deep interior, engine and exterior cleaning as well as any necessary leather refurbishing, bumper repair or wheel restoration.

Reconditioning fees are legal, but that doesn’t mean you have to pay them. Dealers can charge whatever they want for a reconditioning fee, and it’s not uncommon to find them ranging from around $500 to over $2,000.

The good news: Reconditioning fees, like most dealer extras, can be negotiable. Ask the dealer for a breakdown of the reconditioning fee to see what kind of work was done to prepare the car for sale. If the work was minor but the fee is high — for example, if they’re asking for $1,995 but replaced the floor mats only — that’s a good sign it’s a bogus charge.

However, when it comes time for negotiation, don’t just haggle over the reconditioning fee or other individual fees. The best way to negotiate is to focus on the out-the-door price, or the total amount you’re paying for the car. This simplifies the negotiation process and keeps you focused on the true price of your car.

All dealers invest some amount in reconditioning, but those costs are often built into the dealer’s asking price for a vehicle, rather than as a separate charge.

Some dealers opt to add a reconditioning fee to the bill because it allows them to advertise a lower asking price online, which doesn’t include fees. It’s a tactic to get you into their dealership, since the asking price is lower than that of other dealerships.

However, once you arrive, you may find that the out-the-door price is the same — or more — compared with what other dealers are asking.

Some car-buying experts recommend avoiding dealerships that charge reconditioning fees altogether. But remember that the out-the-door price is what matters at the end of the day, so it’s worth comparing prices at multiple dealerships to find the best deal.

Reddit is an online forum where users share their thoughts in “threads” on various topics. The popular site includes plenty of discussion on financial subjects like car buying, so we sifted through Reddit threads to get a sense of how users feel about reconditioning fees. People post anonymously, so we cannot confirm their individual experiences or circumstances.

Reddit users are generally skeptical toward reconditioning fees. Many users view these fees as a bait-and-switch tactic: dealers advertise an attractive price online and then tack on the fee at the dealership, inflating the actual out-the-door cost well above what was listed. Car buyers frequently share stories of being hit with reconditioning fees ranging from a few hundred dollars up to $2,000 or more, often with little or no evidence of what work was actually done.

Car-buying experts on Reddit point out that most dealerships already build reconditioning costs into the sticker price, so if the fee is listed separately, the dealership may be double-dipping. Several users suggest that car buyers ask their dealer for an itemized breakdown of the work done, but most users recommend negotiating the total out-the-door price rather than individual fees.

This Sunday's game at U.S. Bank Stadium between the Atlanta Falcons and the Minnesota Vikings will feature Kirk Cousins' return to the Twin Cities as the Vikings' quarterback to take on the team he left Minnesota for before the 2024 season. It's one of the more interesting storylines of Week 12 of this NFL season, and a pretty significant chunk of the country will be able to see it on over-the-air television.

The folks from 506 Sports have released their broadcast maps for this Week 12, and the FOX map for this week is below. The Falcons/Vikings game is represented by the red area.

There's your broadcast coverage map for Week 12, folks. Hopefully there are quite a few of you who are in the red area on the map for this week's game.

#3000. What Are Repositioning Cruises?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Repositioning cruises move ships between seasonal destinations, often with longer sailings, fewer port stops and lower fares.

A repositioning cruise is a one-way sailing that moves a ship from one region to another. For example, many ships sail in the Mediterranean Sea during the summer and move to the Caribbean Sea for the winter, and that trip between the two is a repositioning cruise.

Repositioning cruises are most common in spring (roughly March to May) and fall (roughly September to November), though they can occur at other times of year.

Because ships are crossing oceans, these trips often include many days at sea. For some travelers, that’s a chance to relax and enjoy the ship’s onboard amenities. Sometimes, these sailings can also be cheaper than traditional cruises.

One key consideration for a repositioning cruise is that the embarkation and disembarkation ports will be different. This means you generally can’t buy a standard round-trip flight to and from the same city. Instead, you’ll need transportation to where the cruise starts and another way home from where it ends, which usually means two one-way flights (or one open-jaw ticket) unless you live near one of the ports. While these one-way sailings may be more affordable than traditional cruises, airfare costs may be higher.

This could be a great opportunity to redeem miles for your flight.

Another feature that sets these cruises apart is that stops along the way tend to include ports that aren’t traditionally part of regular itineraries. These might include islands like Bermuda, the Azores or the Canary Islands, and on some northern routes, Greenland.

Just keep in mind that the main purpose of these sailings is to get to a new region for the next season’s itineraries rather than make daily port stops. So if you’re someone who wants to visit as many places as possible on a cruise, a repositioning sailing may not be the ideal choice.

Repositioning cruises are often longer than standard cruises, commonly running two to three weeks compared with about a week for a typical sailing, which may appeal to travelers looking for an extended vacation. The experience is often more relaxed, with many days at sea and less of a rush to book shore excursions or visit important sites when off the ship.

If there are particular ships with certain amenities — like tattoo parlors or massive waterparks — that you really want to experience, keep in mind that not all ships reposition between regions. Some stay in certain regions year-round.

For ships that do change locations for the season, there are plenty of onboard activities to fill the time. You can enjoy shows, food and wine tastings, organized activities and time at the spa, pool or casino.

Many repositioning crossings are relatively smooth, and modern ships use stabilizers to reduce movement. But since these itineraries cross open ocean rather than staying closer to land, there can be more motion, especially on North Atlantic crossings in fall and winter.

If you tend to get seasick, this could be a dealbreaker. Even if a repositioning sailing is smooth, temperatures can be chilly on the open ocean, especially on northerly or spring and fall transatlantic routes, with strong winds that may make suntanning by the pool less appealing.

You can book one of these sailings in the same way you would book a traditional cruise. But if you’re searching for dates and itineraries on a cruise line’s website, they may be listed as transatlantic, transpacific or Panama Canal sailings rather than repositioning cruises, though some lines do use the term "repositioning." There’s even a website that specializes in rounding up these cruises: RepoCruises.com.

Keep an eye out for themed sailings that focus on topics like wine, theater or music, where fellow guests on the ship may share similar interests. Cruise lines often schedule them on repositioning cruises to spur demand.

Here are some repositioning cruises for 2027. They often take place during shoulder seasons, between the most popular cruising times in places like the Mediterranean or Caribbean.

This journey repositions the ship from Miami, where it sails on Caribbean itineraries, to Seattle, where it will visit ports in Alaska during the spring and summer months. Departing April 8, 2027, the 18-night cruise includes stops at various ports in Colombia, Panama and Mexico, as well as San Diego and Los Angeles, before arriving in Seattle on April 26.

Regent Seven Seas Cruises' all-suite luxury ship Seven Seas Mariner will sail between New York and Lisbon, making stops in Canada, Morocco and the Portuguese islands of Madeira and the Azores.

Departing April 29, 2027, the 16-night journey ends in Lisbon, Portugal, as the ship repositions to Europe, where it will sail various itineraries, including Mediterranean sailings later in the summer season.

Departing Seattle on Sept. 28, 2027, Norwegian Jade will sail to Yokohama, Japan, with stops in Alaska and northern Japan. This is a way to travel across the Pacific with less jet lag, since the journey takes place over several days, with ship clocks typically adjusted gradually (often an hour at a time) during the sailing. Note that the voyage also crosses the International Date Line.

If Greenland has been on your bucket list, this 16-night sailing is a way to get up close and personal with its natural scenery. Departing Reykjavik on Aug. 25, 2027, Azamara Quest will sail from Iceland to the U.S. It will make stops in Greenland, Canada and New England before reaching the Big Apple.

Departing Bergen, Norway, on June 11, 2027, this 28-night sailing aboard Viking Vela sails around Iceland's coast, makes two stops in Greenland and crosses the Atlantic before reaching New York.

Flexibility, point transfers and a large bonus: Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card

#2998. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 24: Lower, But …

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates are down this morning, but could seesaw back up tomorrow.

Another day, another substantial swing. This one's lower, but don't count on it starting a trend: We keep seeing mortgage rates move back and forth, but no one's expecting rates to take a dive. If you're watching this space because you're waiting to refinance, it's unlikely your wait is about to end.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.13% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 25 basis points lower than yesterday and unchanged from a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

The amount of day-to-day discrepancy we've been seeing prompted me to take a little cruise around the web this morning to see what 30-year rates lenders are advertising. Mortgage lenders' rates are never identical, but when we're seeing rapid change, the gaps between lenders tend to widen — and that's for sure what I saw. I looked at six different lenders, and saw a nearly 70-basis-point difference between the highest and lowest APRs. (And yes, that was looking for the same loan amount, location and so on.)

My point is, whether you're looking to buy or refinance, make sure to shop multiple lenders. You're the same candidate bringing the same financials to each one, but you could end up with extremely different rate quotes. And it pays to compare. Freddie Mac estimates that comparing at least two lenders could save you up to $600 per year in interest. Compare four or more, and that savings is over $1,200 annually.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context. For more, keep reading below the charts.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Mortgage rates may be lower today, but like I mentioned above, it's been more of a seesaw than a ski slope — and we may be about to hit the high side again. The latest Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index is due out tomorrow morning. Recently, when rates have moved higher, inflation's been in the mix.

Strong inflation's generally bad for borrowing, including to buy a home, because higher interest rates are the remedy. Raising the federal funds rate (the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers of the Federal Reserve actually set) is the Fed's main tactic for managing inflation. While the Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, its decisions influence the entire economy. That includes mortgage lenders, who tend to adjust rates ahead of its decisions.

We already know how PCE's likely to go, since it's the last of the three big inflation measures to be released. We've already gotten last month's Consumer Price Index and Producer Price Index. October's CPI came in worse than predicted, showing widespread price increases. (When most of the increase is driven by one area, it's easier to be optimistic that inflation can be contained.) And October’s PPI showed wholesale inflation climbed at its fastest pace in almost four years. PPI measures the price businesses pay for things, so it's often a preview of what consumers could face once those costs get passed along.

Predictions for October PCE aren't great, so at this point the hope is that the numbers aren't even worse than expected. And again, PCE's out tomorrow morning. We'll know whether we're getting seconds (actually, thirds) of bad October inflation news well before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Markets are currently anticipating a quarter-point hike at the Fed's December meeting. Even though that'll remain the most likely outcome, a poor showing from PCE could spark speculation about a half-point hike. That probably won't be good for mortgage rates.

But back to the upcoming holiday: Markets will be closed on Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, so we'll all have a bit of a break this week. Mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.63% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

In addition to market factors outside of your control, your customized quote depends on your:

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

#2993. How to Get Grants for College

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Grants don’t have to be repaid, but the money can only go toward school-related costs. Here’s how to use grants to pay for school and how to make them go further.

Grants are one of the best ways you can pay for your education. It’s free money you aren’t expected to pay back.

Grants can be used for tuition, course materials and other cost-of-attendance expenses. Some grants, depending on who awards them, require an application or have requirements you must meet before you can get your money.

Here’s how to use a college grant and what it can pay for.

Federal grants come from the federal government.

State grants come from state programs and often can only be used at schools in that state.

Institutional grants come from your college or university, or even your program or major.

Private grants come from nonprofits, community organizations and individual donors.

To receive a federal grant, you must complete the Free Application for Federal Student Aid (FAFSA). Most states require the FAFSA for state grants, and a few require a separate state application. You’ll need to complete the FAFSA for each year you’re enrolled in school. You may need to meet certain eligibility requirements, such as income limits, to get some grants. The FAFSA is how you apply for the Pell Grant, which is a financial need-based grant. The FAFSA also determines eligibility for federal work-study, which is a job program rather than a grant.

Federal aid, including federal grants, typically requires students to meet satisfactory academic progress. This means you have to maintain a minimum grade point average and be on track to finish your degree on time, as determined by your school. Your school also sets how many credits you must take to qualify for grant funding.

Some grants — typically private or institutional grants — require an additional application that asks for transcripts, letters of recommendation and personal essays. Your school’s financial aid office or the grant provider will have details on applying for specific grants.

After you submit the FAFSA, you’ll receive a financial aid award letter from each school you applied to, which outlines how much in federal, state and institutional grants you’ve been offered. You must accept your aid to receive it.

Most grants will be disbursed directly to your college or university. Your school will apply the grant money first to tuition, fees and any other charges it bills, such as housing or a meal plan.

In some cases, you may receive more grant money than your school bills, which is the best-case scenario. If that happens to you, any leftover funds will likely be refunded to you. Refund timelines vary by school, so it may take some time to get your money. You can get your refund faster by setting up direct deposit with your school. You can then use the funds for other costs approved by your grant — some examples are detailed below.

If you have questions about your grant money, contact your college’s financial aid office.

Grants can help cover cost-of-attendance expenses. Generally, you can use grant money to pay for tuition, textbooks, class materials, transportation, housing and food. Depending on your school’s policies, you can use grant money to cover summer classes or study abroad programs, too.

Some grants come with specific terms about how you can spend them. Check your financial aid award letter or, for a private or institutional grant, the school’s or organization’s website to find specifics.

After your grant is applied to your tuition and school expenses, you can options to fill the gaps between what the grant covers and the total cost of your program. Some options include savings, scholarships, work-study, payment plans, federal student loans or, as a last resort, private loans. By using grants to pay for your college education, you may be able to borrow less.

To maximize the grants you’re eligible to receive, enter accurate information on the FAFSA and complete it as soon as you can — some funds are first-come, first-served. Also, don’t limit yourself to federal or state grants — search and apply for as many privately funded grants as you can find.

Your grant money usually goes straight to your college or university to pay your tuition and fees. Anything that is left over may be disbursed to you for other school-related costs.

Yes, but it depends on your grant. Housing is part of your cost of attendance, so you can use grant money to pay for off-campus rent.

You don’t have to pay back a grant as long as you continue to meet all requirements for the grant, such as staying enrolled. You can use grant money on school and school-related expenses. Some grants may require you to meet eligibility requirements such as a minimum GPA or state residency. If you receive more grant money than your cost of attendance for a given school term, ask your financial aid office if you can put that grant money toward next year’s cost of attendance.

If you have a federal grant like a Pell Grant, these are examples when you may have to repay all or part of the grant:

You received enough scholarship money to reduce your federal aid need.

It depends. You can usually use grant money to buy a laptop. Computers are considered course materials, which fall under cost-of-attendance expenses, so they can be covered by a grant. However, always double-check with the grant provider and your financial aid award letter to ensure that you’re following all restrictions. Whether you can use grant money to cover a laptop depends on your school’s policies and how much grant money you receive.

If you get more grant money than your school bills you, you’ll likely receive the leftover money to spend on other cost-of-attendance expenses. You can use this money on living costs such as transportation or housing. Grant money often goes mostly toward tuition, so a refund isn’t guaranteed.

Grant and scholarship money is generally tax-free when you use it for tuition, required fees, books and required class supplies. If you have additional questions, consider talking to a tax professional.

#2992. NFL honors John Madden with Thanksgiving-themed coins, Madden uniform patch for Thursday slate

Five years after the death of John Madden, the NFL is keeping the memory of the broadcaster's connection to Thanksgiving alive.

The league announced last week that a Madden coin, featuring Madden's silhouette on one side and a six-legged "turducken" (a chicken stuffed in a duck, stuffed in a turkey) on the other, will be used for coin flips for the three NFL games on Thanksgiving Day.

All players in those games — Detroit Lions-Chicago Bears, Dallas Cowboys-Philadelphia Eagles and Buffalo Bills-Kansas City Chiefs — will wear patches on their uniforms with the Madden silhouette. The Los Angeles Rams and Green Bay Packers, who played on Wednesday (or "Thanksgiving Eve," as the league calls it), also wore the patches.

Stencils honoring Madden will be placed in the end zones and along the sidelines of the three home fields during the matchup, and panels on the stadium wall will feature Madden in his coaching days with the Oakland Raiders.

Along with those Madden-themed additions, the MVP of each game will receive a trophy fashioned to look like a turkey leg-football amalgamation. The Thanksgiving MVPs will also earn a $10,000 donation from the NFL Foundation to a high school or youth football program in their name.

Madden, one of the defining figures of the sport as both a coach and broadcaster, died in 2021 at the age of 85. Alongside longtime broadcasting partner Pat Summerall, Madden called NFL games for decades on CBS and later Fox, becoming associated with Thanksgiving games thanks to his enthusiasm for turduckens and turkey legs.

The league first introduced the specialized Madden patches and coins in 2023, two years after the football legend's death, and have continued the tradition over the past few years.

#2989. Mortgage Rates Today, Wednesday, November 25: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Rising inflation could, counterintuitively, help push mortgage rates further down.

Mortgage rates are a little lower this morning, and where they'll go next isn't totally clear.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 7.05% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is eight basis points lower than yesterday and 33 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Inflation data out this morning looks, at first glance, like bad news for mortgage rates. But depending on how markets react, we could actually see lower mortgage rates. Keep reading after the charts for all the details.

Below are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for perspective.

P.S. Markets will be closed tomorrow for Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, and mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Like I mentioned above, it's not totally obvious where mortgage rates will head next. Let's walk through the two scenarios.

Here's why rates could go up: This morning, we got October's Personal Consumption Expenditures price index, also known as PCE. It's the last measure of inflation to come out each month, but it's also the Federal Reserve's preferred metric, so PCE gets a lot of attention.

The stats came in mostly as predicted, though it's not like the predictions were good — inflation's been overly strong for a while. But the core year-over-year figure, which came in a tenth of a percent over predictions, could prove troublesome. Core PCE removes categories that can be extra volatile, so it's considered a measure that shows the underlying trend. In this case, the underlying trend is Not Good.

It's already pretty much a lock that we'll see a rate hike at the Federal Reserve's December meeting, because yes, raising the federal funds rate is the Fed's main tool for taming inflation. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but a rate-hiking cycle from the central bankers definitely keeps upward pressure on borrowing costs.

On the other hand, here's why that same inflation data could help rates go down: Over the past several days, we've watched the stock market go on a wild ride, with the Anthropic IPO and the return of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Yesterday, things weren't looking good for tech stocks, and these inflation numbers could make it even worse.

The amount that tech companies have borrowed to fund AI buildout is a significant concern for investors, since companies will have to cover those costs before investors see any profits. It's already a big task, and if everything costs more thanks to inflation, it's that much harder.

This AI-related anxiety is rough on the stock market, but it's been a boon for Treasury bonds. More demand for bonds pushes up bond prices, which brings down bond yields (basically, the return investors get on a bond). Mortgage rates for 30-year loans are generally benchmarked to the yield on the 10-year Treasury note, so here's why you care about all of this: Lower Treasury yields means lower mortgage rates, too. If investors keep stressing about inflation eating into tech profits, we could see mortgage rates continue to move lower.

That was a lot of info on inflation, but inflation's not the only major metric influencing rates.

Let's back up for a second. The Federal Reserve tries to keep the economy steady by focusing on two key goals, often referred to as the Fed's dual mandate. One side is price stability — keeping inflation reasonable. The other is maximum employment, meaning those who want to work are able to find jobs.

Next week we'll shift our focus to the employment side of the Federal Reserve's mandate, because the first week of the month is always all about employment data. On Tuesday, Dec. 1, we'll kick things off with the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey (JOLTS). JOLTS can tip us off to trends in the labor market; stuff like whether folks are leaving jobs voluntarily or if companies have openings that are going unfilled.

On Wednesday, payroll firm ADP will release its monthly employment stats. ADP is looking at its own payroll data to get an idea of how the job market is growing or contracting. The numbers don't include the public sector, but ADP often provides a preview of how the jobs report will go.

The Employment Situation Summary, better known as the jobs report, drops on Friday. This is the big one, since it tells us the nation's unemployment rate. The jobs report's other major stat is, similar to ADP, how many jobs the economy added or lost in the previous month. Unlike ADP though, the jobs report is inclusive of everybody in the public and private sectors.

Lately the labor market's been looking super healthy, which is great news for the economy but less positive for mortgage rates. When hiring's strong, the Fed can feel safer raising the federal funds rate (that's the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers adjust). Higher rates tend to discourage businesses from borrowing and expanding, so if the job market's on shaky footing, the central bankers are going to be cautious about raising the benchmark rate.

What we're seeing now though, where inflation is clearly the problem and employment's just fine, is a recipe for higher rates. Again, the Fed doesn't control mortgage rates, but the central bank being in rate-hike mode puts upward pressure on mortgage rates.

If we were to see employment falter in this latest round of numbers, it still probably wouldn't change the Fed's plan. That would be one poor month for the labor market versus months upon months of inflation running rampant — so yeah, they'll keep an eye on jobs, but inflation's still the scourge.

Our most likely source of lower mortgage rates in the short term is what I described above; investors who are leery of inflation and the promise of AI looking to the safe harbor of the bond market.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.55% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

In addition to market factors outside of your control, your customized quote depends on your:

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

» Get the best rate for you: How to get the best mortgage rate

Maybe — but even personalized rate quotes can change until you lock. That’s because lenders adjust pricing multiple times a day in response to market changes.

#2987. LaMelo Ball injury update: Timberwolves star hurt late in win vs. Thunder

The Minnesota Timberwolves were already without their biggest star due to injury. Now they may have lost their second-best player, at least for the short term.

Late in Minnesota's game Wednesday, Nov. 25 against the Oklahoma City Thunder, with 2:29 left in the fourth quarter, point guard LaMelo Ball was defending a play in the middle of the paint, when he jumped and landed awkwardly, rolling his left ankle. Ball immediately grabbed at his foot and writhed on the floor in obvious discomfort. He remained on the floor for several moments before being helped toward the bench.

Trainers assessed Ball's foot while he sat on the end of the bench. After some minutes, he eventually got up and limped toward the locker room.

"No," Timberwolves coach Chris Finch told reporters after the game when asked if he had an update on Ball. "It looked pretty bad, but I'm not sure."

The Athletic reported, however, that Ball was in good spirits in the postgame locker room and was laughing and joking with his teammates.

Ball scored 19 points on 7-of-18 shooting before leaving the game, adding 8 assists and 7 rebounds.

Ball is an absence the Timberwolves cannot afford. The team has been without All-Star Anthony Edwards, the most dynamic shooting guard in the NBA, since the fifth game of the regular season. Edwards underwent an arthroscopic procedure on his knee to clean up the area and reduce swelling.

Despite Edwards' absence, the Timberwolves have thrived, sprinting out to a 14-5 record, good for third place in the Western Conference.

Guard Ayo Dosunmu and forward Jaden McDaniels have also had a big offensive impact with Edwards sidelined, and if Ball is forced to miss some time, even more will be asked of the pair.

The Timberwolves would eventually beat the Thunder in their game, 113-106.

#2984. Ready for ‘The Great Lock In’? Viral Money Trend Can Improve Focus

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Winter is the ideal time to make progress on your financial goals.

When it’s cold outside, it’s the ideal time to stay home, organize your finances, set some new money goals and make progress toward them.

That’s the promise of the viral winter money trend known as “The Great Lock In” that’s circulating on social media.

“You’re probably not doing too much,” says Robert Baird, wealth manager at Cornerstone Financial Services in Southfield, Michigan. “It gets dark early. So those can be great months to lock into saving and not spend in excess.”

One important component of the Great Lock In is developing new routines that will last well into 2027, says Jordan Mangaliman, founder and fiduciary wealth advisor at GoldLine Wealth Management in Fullerton, California.

“We want a long-term habit versus a Band-Aid fix,” he says. “In today’s economy, money goes in and out so blindingly fast.”

Ready to join the movement? Here’s how to get started.

What’s your money moonshot? Maybe it’s paying off all your credit card debt or purchasing a home. Or spending less than you earn. Defining it is the first step toward achieving it.

“Spend a lot of time identifying what that goal is,” Baird says. “Everyone’s in a different position, so comparing yourself to what you see online might not be applicable.”

Even while setting ambitious money goals, it’s worth grounding them in reality, Mangaliman says.

If you want to save a larger portion of your income, he suggests trying the new saving pattern for a couple of months to see how it feels rather than saying you’ll do it forever and then getting frustrated.

“It’s a marathon, not a sprint,” Mangaliman adds.

Once you’ve outlined those big goals for your Great Lock In, create a budget to help you achieve them, Baird says.

He suggests combing through your previous credit or debit card spending to see where your money is going. Then you can decide what you want to change or shift. Using a tool like the 50/30/20 budget calculator can also help. This approach allots 50% of take-home pay toward needs, 30% toward wants and 20% toward savings and debt payments beyond the minimum.

Baird suggests including saving as a line item in your budget so you don’t forget to do it. “Building that cost into your budget will put you in a better position later,” he says.

Any Great Lock In should include growing your savings, because that makes achieving other goals easier, Baird says.

“Maybe there’s something you could cut in the short term to build up that savings,” he says. Good targets include subscription services, food delivery, entertainment and travel costs.

Saving more is especially challenging in today’s environment, when everyday expenses like gas are higher than they were a year ago.

Baird says that makes winter a good time to try to reduce costs. There can be fewer temptations to go out and spend money when the days are shorter and the temperatures are colder.

Mangaliman recommends setting aside three to six months’ worth of expenses, although even a smaller amount is helpful. “Ultimately it’s about what you can afford,” he says. “It’s the foundation of your financial house.”

Along with building savings, paying down high-interest debt is another pillar of the Great Lock In.

With credit card interest rates higher than they were a few years ago, carrying credit card debt has gotten more expensive. “I would consider applying some savings to paying down debt, especially right now when your debt is costing you more than you can earn in savings,” Baird says. (You still want to reserve an emergency fund to cover unexpected expenses.)

“High-interest debt is one of the biggest thieves of wealth,” Mangaliman says. And one way to avoid turning to debt again in the future is to build up that emergency fund so it’s there for you if you need it.

Even when you are committed to a Great Lock In, some hiccups along the way are inevitable. Success depends on continuing anyway.

“It’s like a diet,” Baird says. “Just because you ate ice cream on a Sunday doesn’t mean on Monday you can’t go right back to being disciplined again.”

“Be kind to yourself and do everything in moderation,” Mangaliman suggests. Just wanting to improve your finances is already a great first step, he says.

In fact, he embraces the Great Lock In mentality himself. “There’s an old saying: It’s not how you start, but how you finish,” he says. “And you want to finish the year on a strong note so you carry those habits into 2027 and beyond.”

#2978. 4 Ways to Prep Your Finances Before the Next Tech Outage

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

You don’t need a basement bunker, but keeping some cash on hand might help.

If your social media feed is anything like mine, you’ve probably seen a reel or two on nonperishable food and solar-powered generators after the recent string of tech outages.

Have I considered giving water-filtering straws as stocking stuffers this year? Maybe.

But what about your money? If your area was hit by a natural disaster or your bank had an outage, are your finances ready?

Dan Stanush, senior financial planner at Charles Schwab in Asheville, North Carolina, recalls how fast financial conveniences disappeared after Hurricane Helene hit in 2024. “We lost power for three weeks and water for six weeks,” he said in an email. “And some local businesses could only accept cash or checks because card systems and ATMs were down.”

Don’t get caught unprepared. Here are the moves experts suggest to help you weather various types of emergencies:

Last week’s Chase outage was a good reminder that access to your bank balance can be disrupted. Stash at least enough cash at home — in small bills — to cover one week of expenses, says Jeff Judge, a certified financial planner (CFP) with Chesapeake Financial Planners in Forest Hill, Maryland.

If systems go down, ATMs can go down with them, said Eileen Olson, a CFP with Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, in an email. Keeping cash in a safe at home is a good plan, but it’s also smart to carry cash when you travel. (Remember when planes were grounded nationwide a week and a half ago?)

“My parents and sister were traveling overseas on 9/11 and got stuck much longer than they expected,” Olson said. “After that, they always traveled with more cash.”

If your primary bank and its app were down, would you be able to pay bills? Having an account at more than one bank gives you an alternate funding source. Consider opening a high-yield savings account at a second bank or credit union for your emergency fund.

“This is the step I push hardest,” Judge says. “I had a client whose debit card, credit card and savings all ran through one bank, so when that bank’s system went down, she couldn’t even buy gas.”

If you don’t want to open a second bank account, a second credit card from a different bank and preferably on a different network (i.e. Visa vs. Mastercard) can provide another way to pay for things. “If there’s a problem with one credit card network, I have a backup with two other credit cards,” says Tony Steuer, author of “Get Ready! A Step-by-Step Planner for Maintaining Your Financial First Aid Kit.”

Make a printed list of your accounts, along with account numbers and key contact numbers. Include your bank, insurers, credit card companies and any financial professionals. Store the list with copies of essential paperwork, including insurance policies and their declarations pages, estate planning documents, legal certificates and identification — potentially in a fire-resistant file box.

Keep a digital copy of your documents in a password-protected, encrypted spot as well. A password manager can store all of your login info.

Joon Um, a CFP with Secure Tax & Accounting in Beverly Hills, California, suggests digital storage site Everplans to his clients, but there are a variety of sites that offer encrypted document storage.

Take some time to write down how you’d pay for groceries, gas and medication if your usual payment methods weren’t available. What expenses would you need to cover first? What recurring bills are on autopay? Where would you keep the cash and any key documents you’d want to take with you if you had to leave home? Make sure other household members know where everything is.

And set a reminder to update your records at regular intervals. Steuer, for instance, saves his account statements as PDFs and prints everything out once a year. “One of the big concerns is that people could conceivably break into your 401(k) and drain your 401(k) account,” he says. Account statements could help if a hack affects any financial balances.

It all adds up to a web of protection that (hopefully) you’ll never have to use.

“The goal is not to anticipate every scenario,” said Schwab's Dan Stanush, “but to have enough flexibility to meet basic needs while systems come back online.”

#2975. Knicks rally to beat Cavaliers behind unlikely hero, expanded lineup to keep slim NBA Cup hopes alive

Mike Brown coached Wednesday's NBA Cup contest like a preseason game. He used a 12-man rotation, throwing out Tyler Nickel for his NBA debut and giving important minutes to sparsely used Pacôme Dadiet.

Brown, as he noted before tipoff, sees the scheduling benefits of being eliminated from Adam Silver's beloved tournament.

But it didn't matter. The Knicks (12-6), winners of six straight overall, surged in the fourth quarter behind unlikely hero Jose Alvarado.

Mikal Bridges celebrates after hitting a 3-pointer during the Knicks' 121-102 win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 25, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

They avoided Cup elimination — at least for a couple of days — with a 121-102 win over the collapsing Cavaliers, who blew an 11-point lead late in the third quarter.

The Knicks (1-2 in group play) remain unlikely to advance. To win the group, they'd need a victory over the Heat on Friday by at least seven points and a Sixers loss to the Pacers. Advancing as the wild card is more complicated and unlikely.

But the defending Cup champs aren't technically eliminated as of Thanksgiving.

They were short-handed Wednesday without OG Anunoby (calf strain) and Miles McBride (left adductor tightness). They lost backup center Andre Drummond to a head injury in the first half.

Then they punished the Cavs (9-10) by winning the fourth quarter 39-17 with Alvarado scoring 16 of his 21 points in the final period and Brown going deep into his bench.

"I looked at the bench in the first quarter and realized, 'Wow, we played like 10 people in that first quarter,' " said Josh Hart, who had 15 points and 11 assists.

Jose Alvarado celebrates after hitting a second 3-pointer in the fourth quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers. Jason Szenes for NY Post

Mikal Bridges finished with an efficient 19 points. Karl-Anthony Towns extended his double-double streak to 15 (barely) with 12 points and 10 boards.

If and when Brown's squad is out of the tournament, the coach is looking at elimination as an NBA Cup half full. Losing translates to an easier schedule for the Knicks, which wasn't lost on the coach before Wednesday's comeback win.

Brown knows because the NBA shared the possibilities. Advancement potentially brings another three road games — including the final in Indiana.

Josh Hart reacts during the second quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

And elimination means two games — including one at home — over a nine-day stretch.

"You look at it and you're like, 'Whoa, hell, that's a lot of days off if you don't advance,' " Brown said. "And so, again, you wanna compete, you wanna win, but if you don't … there's an opportunity if you got some guys banged up to get rest and or to work on some things in between the games that you have if you're not involved in the Cup."

Silver won't like that answer from the defending NBA Cup champs. But Brown was speaking from experience.

The Knicks added a long road trip to their schedule last season because of the NBA Cup, then an 83rd game with the final in Las Vegas.

The results were phenomenal for the Knicks. They won the Cup, earned the prize money (each player won over $500,000) and months later captured the big NBA championship.

But the Knicks, as an organization, didn't fully embrace Silver's tournament. It took them nearly a full year to hang the banner.

Then Wednesday, the coach of the Cup champs was openly espousing the virtues of being eliminated.

Mikal Bridges rises up for a layup during the second quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

No crying over a spilled NBA Cup. For now, though, the Cup is still on the table.

"When I talk about the Cup, it adds a level of stress maybe on your mind and your body because the games are intense as you go along and you're playing more games in a short amount of time," Brown said. "I remember last year it was like, 'Game, Game, Game.' And then you win the Cup and you celebrate it that night and you hop on a plane and go play in Indiana the next night. And so it's great. You like the competition and the setup but it adds a lot of stress to you physically and mentally. I'm saying this because especially with the extended season we had last year and how taxing the games were for our playoff run mentally and physically, I'm trying to be smart."

#2974. How Much Is Jensen Huang Worth to Nvidia? The Stock Market Just Gave a Clear Answer

Jensen Huang’s return sent Nvidia’s stock soaring.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 26, 2026 at 7:34AM EST

Jensen Huang's saga just took another unexpected turn. Nvidia's (NVDA -3.91%) board unanimously voted to reinstate him as CEO, less than a week after explicitly saying it wouldn't. The message the board is sending is clear. Despite Huang's extramarital affairs, including with an alleged Chinese spy and with one of his subordinates, he is extremely valuable to the company. Just how valuable? The market's reaction to Huang's reinstatement gives us a clue.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

It's no secret that Wall Street loves founder-led businesses, but why is that? There are several reasons. Let's discuss two of them. First, founders, who often own a substantial portion of the company, have a strong incentive to maximize shareholder value. Hired CEOs do too, to some extent. That's why equity-based compensation exists.

However, founders tend to have more skin in the game. They wouldn't just lose their job if their corporation failed, unlike many hired CEOs. They would lose a substantial portion of their wealth. Second, founders often have a much deeper knowledge of the business they created, its characteristics, risks, competitive advantages, etc. That may give them an edge in leading the business to greater heights. According to some research, founder-led companies generally outperform broader equities.

So, Wall Street's faith isn't blind -- it is backed by data. That brings us back to Huang. He helped found Nvidia in 1993 and made it one of the most valuable companies in the world, and the corporation at the center of the ongoing artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. Huang is one of the longest-serving founder-CEOs in corporate America. That counts for a lot

That's why the stock soared about 31%, gaining nearly $1 trillion in a single session, after it was announced that he was coming back. It was the largest single-day market-cap gain in history.

Huang's return reduces the uncertainty tied to Nvidia's CEO search. Before the announcement, the market didn't know whether it would take six weeks or six months, and it was hard to reliably assign probability estimates to each outcome. The market abhors uncertainty, so the news had a major calming effect for many investors. That doesn't mean that Nvidia is out of trouble.

The company still faces major investigations tied to Huang's scandals, and the man is scheduled to testify before Congress soon. Still, with Huang at the helm -- and even accounting for ongoing regulatory probes -- the stock looks attractive. The first sign of that is Nvidia's financial results. In the third quarter of its fiscal year 2027, which ended on Oct. 25, Nvidia crushed expectations.

The company's revenue soared 103% year over year to $115.8 billion, with data center revenue rising 110% compared to the year-ago period. Nvidia's non-GAAP gross margin remains exceptional for a company of this size at 74.9%, versus 73.5% in the year-ago period. Nvidia's non-GAAP earnings per share jumped 111% year over year to $2.68.

The company's AI tailwind is not slowing down much, if at all. It expects $130 billion in revenue for its fiscal year 2027 fourth quarter at the midpoint, representing a year-over-year increase of about 91%. Sure, that's a lower year-over-year revenue growth rate than in its recent third quarter, and so is Nvidia's 2028 fiscal year guidance of 75% year-over-year revenue growth.

But investors should remember two things. First, Nvidia tends to post stronger-than-expected results. It has done so more often than not for the better part of three years. So, a predicted 91% year-over-year growth rate may turn into something above 100% -- in fact, the company's Q3 2027 guidance implied its revenue would increase by about 89.5% compared to the year-ago period.

Further, a decelerating growth rate may indicate tougher year-over-year comparisons rather than slowing demand for the company's products. Nvidia clearly still has a large market to tap into, and with Huang back at the helm, it is as well-positioned as ever to do so. And even after its 31% jump, the stock is down about 36% from the all-time high reached a little over a month ago. At current levels, Nvidia stock remains a buy.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 777%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 199% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.
