#2939. Nano-X Imaging: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Nano-X's cash runway depends on whether commercial execution can catch up with its technology before reserves run thin.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 10:28AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Nano-X Imaging ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 25, 2026, Nano-X Imaging (NNOX -12.28%) announced revenue of $4.4 million. Management is pivoting from a medical-screening-as-a-service (MSaaS) pay-per-scan model to upfront equipment sales to accelerate cash generation. Investors must weigh whether commercial execution can catch up with technological development before cash reserves run thin. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Nano-X is shifting away from its medical-screening-as-a-service (MSaaS) pay-per-scan contract model toward capital expenditure (CapEx) upfront sales to bolster cash flow immediately. Commercial adoption has lagged technological development, forcing management to prioritize near-term liquidity.

The company opened its first Nanox Imaging Network (NIN) location in Philadelphia, establishing an operational benchmark for specialized clinical deployments. Management expects each network facility to generate recurring revenue through higher reimbursement rates in dedicated care segments.

With cash reserves standing at $26.6 million as of Sept. 30, 2026, Nano-X is cutting operating expenses (OpEx) to extend its financial runway. Management is restructuring international operations and moving chip production to original equipment manufacturer (OEM) partners to reduce costs.

Commercial execution remains the core focus as Nano-X works to convert technical recognition into sustainable revenue. Proprietary digital 3D imaging technology, expanding distribution partnerships, and growing artificial intelligence (AI) software integrations provide structural advantages for future growth. Management is watching channel partner sales performance, system installation timelines, and operating expense reductions.

Financial flexibility and disciplined execution will determine whether the business achieves long-term commercial viability.

#2937. Worried About a Market Crash? 2 Gold Mining Stocks to Buy Before 2026 Ends.

Agnico Eagle Mines and Franco-Nevada are two of the best stocks to buy in advance of a market crash.

By James Halley – Nov 26, 2026 at 10:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Agnico Eagle Mines ›

If you're building a portfolio to survive a potential market crash or economic downturn, Agnico Eagle Mines (AEM +3.03%) and Franco-Nevada (FNV +3.06%) should be on your list. The Toronto-based companies combine exposure to gold, a traditional safe-haven asset, with strong balance sheets that can withstand liquidity crunches.

All gold mining stocks present a relatively high level of risk. The price of gold vacillates wildly. The precious metal hit an all-time high of $5,589.38 per ounce at the end of January but is now trading at around $3,572 per ounce.

It's cost-intensive to run a mining company, and even when gold prices are rising, operational costs and debt loads can easily outweigh a company's profit potential. Agnico Eagle Mines and Franco-Nevada both mitigate those risks, but in substantially different ways.

Agnico Eagle's gold mines present less jurisdictional risk from geopolitical and labor disruptions than typical gold mining stocks. The company operates in Tier-1 mining-friendly locations, including Canada, Australia, Finland, and Mexico.

Agnico also hasn't had to make expensive acquisitions of late thanks to continued underground expansion at its Canadian Malartic location and project growth at Hope Bay in Nunavut, Canada, and at Ikkari in Finland.

In the third quarter, the company reported nine-month all-in sustaining costs (AISC) of $1,529 per ounce of gold while selling each ounce for an average of $4,537. Agnico reported nine-month earnings per share (EPS) of $9.25, up 58% year over year. The stock is up more than 14% so far this year, and even with that gain, the stock is trading at below 13 times earnings.

The company also raised its dividend by 12.5% this year to $0.45 per share quarterly, yielding around 1.11% at its current share price, which is right around the S&P 500 average. The cash dividend payout ratio is only 19.3%, so it is more than well-covered.

Franco-Nevada is a gold streaming company. It doesn't actually mine for gold but earns royalties from the gold-mining companies to which it has provided up-front funds. When gold prices rise, the company benefits either from royalty payments or the right to purchase a portion of the mine's gold production at a pre-agreed-upon discounted price, known as a stream.

The company has interests in 445 assets, including 129 operating mines. Most of its money comes from gold mining, roughly 70%, though it also makes money from natural gas liquids, oil, gas, and silver.

Because it only needs a handful of employees -- 44 full-time and five part-time contractors, as of the end of 2025 -- to operate, its margins are higher than typical mining companies.

Through nine months, the company reported EPS of $6.30, up 63.1% year over year, and record revenue of $1.78 billion, up 45% over the same period a year ago. Adjusted net income margin improved by 710 basis points to 65.8%. Even with those numbers, its shares have fallen more than 7% so far this year.

Franco-Nevada's dividend yields 0.72% at its current share price. The company has raised its quarterly dividend for 19 consecutive years, including a 16% hike this year to $0.44 per share. Like Agnico Eagle, its dividend is well-covered, with a cash dividend payout ratio of only 26.3%.

Gold's price has been weighed down this year by high interest rates, as high-yield bonds have become a more attractive vehicle for passive investing. However, if the stock market declines sharply, rather than raising rates, the Federal Reserve would likely lower them, making gold more attractive.

In the meantime, Agnico Eagle and Franco-Nevada are financially healthy and more stable than most mining stocks, with safe dividends.

#2934. Nets snap a five game losing streak, beat the Bucks 104-97

The Brooklyn Nets and Milwaukee Bucks came into tonight's game like two school kids trotting onto the playground, one pointing and chirping at the other "you're the worst defense," while the other bickered back "no, you're the worst defense."

Each youngster had some merit to their argument. Brooklyn had just given up 130+ in three of their last four games, and ranked dead last in defensive rating ahead of the contest. Milwaukee ranked behind them by a few fractions of a point.

Brooklyn would eventually win the squabble, and the game, but it'd take them some time to get there, not that anyone's complaining here.

Brooklyn came out the gates without a whole lot of ball pressure. In fairness, they had Danny Wolf matched up initially against Tyler Herro, which leaves you very little spatial insurance to work with. However, the Nets also looked out of sync rotating around the Milwaukee offense, which didn't help either. As Brooklyn shot 1-6 from the field and the Bucks pranced out to a 9-2 lead, it just looked like more of the same in the early going.

As I mentioned, we'd eventually find out otherwise. And while real turnaround would soon come on the defensive end, things had to start for Brooklyn at the other.

After Milwaukee's opening jolt, the Nets went on a 13-0 run in the heart of the first to go up 22-12. Michael Porter Jr., who spoke to Brain Lewis this week about how he's struggled to "find his rhythm," was in his grove amid the stretch, putting in eight first period points.

Getting five from Day'Ron Sharpe and Julius Randle each, as well as eight points off the bench in the period, the Nets led after one by a 28-24 margin.

Despite being less of a scorer this time around, Mikel Brown Jr. looked comfortable out there early on as well, starting for a second straight game. He shot 0-5 from the field in the first half but clocked in as a +4 with two assists with zero turnovers.

Also like last game, Grant Nelson joined him on the floor for a few minutes in the second. Nelson only finished with one point, but grabbed six rebounds, contributing to a strong collective effort from the team on the glass, where the Nets won 52-44. Porter Jr. also grabbed 11 rebounds, his most in a game all season if you discount the overtime affair vs Boston.

Brooklyn would need every one of those boards especially in the first half, where neither team shot it at a particularly high clip, as the Nets posted .400/.348 splits, while the Bucks had .432/.273 marks.

At the break, Brooklyn led 51-47, largely thanks to Randle, who had 16 points on 7-10 shooting from the field and 2-4 from deep. MPJ went quiet in the second, still rocking with eight points by then. Day'Ron Sharpe put us on early triple-double watch with five points, 10 rebounds, and four assists at the break.

Herro, however, had all the stats to begin the third. He poured in nine points during the period's first four minutes to put his team back in the lead for the first time since the 6:57 mark of the first. The Nets, meanwhile, opened the period 3-12 from the field and 0-5 from deep. Two of the makes came from Brown Jr, so at least there's that.

Sarcasm aside, as one young guard for Brooklyn started to turn it on, another followed. After Milwaukee looked ready to pull away, Egor Dëmin checked in, scoring five points himself and assisting on two baskets in just a minute and a half. His effort helped ignited a 13-2 run that put the lead back in Brooklyn's hands.

At the same time, Randle continued to mine inside for points. He'd eventually go for 28 in the game on 10-16 shooting. That not only gave Brooklyn consistent offense, but slowed things down and allowed them to set up easily at the other end. Even with Herro's early offensive excellence, the Bucks went 7-23 from the field in the quarter, and the Nets led going into the fourth by a 77-71 score.

"I think everybody was able to just put their minds together and not just focus on team defense, but their own island, like how we say 'guard your yard,'" Dëmin said. "I think that's where it came from. Everything else was easier because everybody was so locked in and super focused on how to guard the man in front of you rather than relying on someone else around."

And believe it or not, in that period, the defense continued to improve. On Milwaukee's second possession of the frame, Porter Jr. poked the a ball away in the backcourt, which Nelson dove across mid court to save. Still, the Bucks got it up and forward, but then Keon Ellis came charging over, intercepting the rock and then dishing it ahead Porter Jr. for an and-one finish. That was just one of the Nets seven steals in the fourth.

"Go out there and play with physicality," Sharpe said. "Go out there and play with ball pressure. Execute with staples and have each others back. I felt like we did a great job at that tonight."

Sharpe never got the triple double, but did finish 11 points, five assists, and 14 rebounds while shooting 5-10 from the field and hitting a three.

The Nets got up by 11 early in the fourth. After a few turnovers early in the frame, the Bucks cut it back down to eight, and we looked in store for a tight finish.

But in a game where Brooklyn's supposed fatal flaw had carried them, it was only right that Noah Clowney, who's struggled with his shot all year, iced things out. In the fourth, he added nine points on 4-6 shooting, scoring from all over the court to keep the lead afloat. He finished with a season-high 17 points, also grabbing five rebounds while shooting 7-13 from the field.

"This is the Noah Clowney that we need," Fernández said. "It gives us a starter off the bench and it's very, very good for this group, because it brings a player on both ends."

Dëmin, who's also had a difficult start to the year, leveraged his size to play some inspiring defense down the stretch as well, finishing as a +5 in the quarter. For the game, he had 15 points, four assists, three rebounds, a steal, and a block while shooting 5-11 from the field.

"Yeah, he went back to doing the simple things, and it started with his physicality on defense," Fernández said. "They felt him the whole game. His length. He can rebound, he can be in the gaps…Proud of him for sure."

Sure, the Nets still turned it over on back-t0-back possession with the Bucks only down 11 and under a minute to go. Dëmin missed also missed an open dunk. They're still a young, flawed team. They didn't solve all their issues tonight, but they did win the night, and snapped their five game losing streak.

Final: Brooklyn Nets 104, Milwaukee Bucks 97

Mo Wagner subbed out at the end of the first period and did not return for the rest of the game. At half, the Nets ruled him out with left knee soreness.

"I don't know. I was about to sub him in, and I saw him limping, and I was like I got to save him from himself," Fernández said. "He was there to do whatever it took for his team and his teammates, and at the end I decided to not bring him back in. Obviously, I don't know what it is, but the good thing, the great thing is he was cheering for everybody. He was cheering fro Grant [Nelson] the whole time, and that means a lot for the group, and it speaks to the type of person he is."

We'll provide updates on him once they're made available.

Day'Ron Sharpe had a season-high 14 rebounds tonight and also recorded his 5th double-double of the season with 11 points. It matches his double-double total from last season, tied as the 2nd most in his career (6, 2023-24). This was his 7th career game with 10+ PTS, 10+ REB and 5+ AST.

Noah Clowney had a season-high 17 points off the bench tonight against the Bucks. He has tied his season high with three 3-pointers made.

Michael Porter Jr. logged his third double-double of the season with 12 points and 11 rebounds. It is his 15th time recording at least 10 rebounds as a Net.

Brooklyn gets right back in action after the holiday with a road matchup against the Toronto Raptors on Friday. The North has lost seven of their last eight, including a contest to the Bucks on Monday. They're certainly looking at this one as a "get right" game. The game tips off at 7:30 p.m. EST.

#2933. Semiconductor Sales Will Soar to $2.6 Trillion in 2027. Buy This Top Stock Before It Skyrockets Next Year

Strong memory demand will push semiconductor sales higher in 2027, and that's great news for Lam Research investors.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 26, 2026 at 10:32AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Lam Research ›

Though semiconductor stocks have taken a beating lately, a closer look at the financial performance of companies in this sector clearly indicates that the sell-off isn't justified.

The semiconductor industry's revenue has been growing at an impressive pace, driven by rising demand for chips used in artificial intelligence (AI) applications, especially in data centers. This explains why the semiconductor industry's revenue in 2026 is anticipated to increase by 111% to $1.8 trillion, according to semiconductor intelligence provider TechInsights.

What's more, TechInsights predicts that the industry's revenue will soar to $2.6 trillion next year. That's why now is a good time to take a closer look at a top semiconductor stock while it trades at an attractive valuation -- Lam Research (LRCX -5.68%) -- before it steps on the gas next year.

TechInsights estimates that the memory market's revenue could hit $1.6 trillion next year, accounting for 61% of the overall semiconductor market. The firm adds that the memory market could clock higher revenue next year if there are no supply constraints.

Not surprisingly, memory manufacturers are ramping up capital expenditures. Micron Technology, for instance, is estimating $25 billion in capex for the first half of fiscal 2027, followed by higher spending in the second half. Micron spent $27.4 billion on capex in fiscal 2026, suggesting its spending could double this year.

Given that memory supply conditions are expected to tighten in 2027 and 2028, according to Micron, and other memory manufacturers expect the shortage to last until 2030, I won't be surprised to see memory capex jump higher in the coming years. This is great news for Lam Research, a semiconductor equipment company that's benefiting from the memory shortage.

Lam Research's revenue in the first quarter of fiscal 2027 shot up by 54% from the year-ago period. The company reported non-GAAP earnings per share (EPS) of $2.20, up 75% from the year-ago period. Investors should note that Lam Research gets 46% of its revenue from sales of memory manufacturing equipment, which explains the impressive growth in revenue and earnings. Specifically, Lam's revenue from sales of memory equipment increased by 120% year over year in the previous quarter.

Even better, Lam's growth is poised to accelerate in the current quarter. The company's $8.6 billion revenue estimate for the current quarter implies a 61% increase over the year-ago period. The adjusted EPS estimate of $2.33 would be an 83% improvement from the same quarter last year. As investments in memory manufacturing equipment increase in 2027 and beyond, Lam should be able to sustain its solid growth rate.

Analysts are forecasting a 66% increase in its EPS in the current fiscal year (which ends in June next year). They are expecting healthy double-digit growth over the next two fiscal years as well, though don't be surprised to see it outperform those expectations due to the tight memory supply, which will encourage manufacturers to invest in more capital equipment.

LRCX EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

In fact, Lam estimates that spending on wafer and fabrication equipment (WFE) could increase from the mid-$150 billion range this year to more than $200 billion in 2027. That implies a potential increase of almost 30%.

Lam management noted on the recent earnings call that it has been gaining market share in the WFE space and sees opportunities for further share gains. So, there is a strong likelihood that Lam's growth will outpace analysts' expectations in 2027 and beyond.

Lam Research stock has lost 53% of its value since the beginning of July. The stock trades at just 19 times forward earnings, only a slight premium to the S&P 500 index's forward earnings multiple of 18.

We have already seen that Lam's EPS is on track to increase by 66% this year, well above the 34% growth that S&P 500 companies are expected to deliver. Analysts expect Lam to outperform the S&P 500's earnings growth next year as well. So, it is easy to see why this semiconductor stock is expected to soar by 89% over the coming year, based on the 12-month median price target of $350, according to CNN.

All this indicates that Lam Research is an AI stock worth buying hand over fist following its recent pullback, as it could step on the gas in 2027 amid strong semiconductor sales.

#2932. Fernando Mendoza perfected Aaron Rodgers 'guitar fake' since joining Raiders

There were few criticisms to be leveed against Fernando Mendoza coming out of college. The Heisman Trophy winner led the Indiana Hoosiers to an undefeated season and a National Championship. But one of the narratives surround his potential success in the pos was the fact that he had worked almost exclusively out of the shotgun and it remained to be seen whether he could transition to working under center at the NFL level.

Not only has he put those questions to bed in his first two starts, he has far exceeded expectations in one aspect in particular -- the play action fake.

"It's been a journey," Mendoza said of his learning curve on play fakes, "especially all the under center fakes and making sure that your play-action fakes look the same as your handoffs, and that's been a big point of emphasis and pride, especially with all the coaches. Coach [Mike] Sullivan has been working with me intently on that throughout this entire process, and I still have a long way to go."

The above play action bootleg resulted in a touchdown pass to TE Ian Thomas. And he had a couple nice play action fakes early in the Week 10 games against the Seahawks to lead out a touchdown drive. Which speaks very highly of his football IQ and work ethic.

"I've seen him make great strides in that area," said head coach Klint Kubiak. "Obviously, not playing a lot of under center football, he was very intentional about it going from April, and then we took time off, and he came back and definitely that was a part of his game that grew when we weren't with him. So obviously, extremely self-motivated to get better in all facets of the game."

The thing is though, that Mendoza's skill in the play action fake is not just proficient, it's already downright elite. His execution of it is being compared to Aaron Rodgers and his signature guitar fake.

"Yeah, Aaron Rogers, he also went to Cal, so it's a little funny, but it was something that we've worked on a lot," Mendoza added. "It's a part of Coach [Klint] Kubiak's scheme, and that's something that we were even talking about after the game, that in April when I first showed up, the guitar fake, it's what we call it, looked horrendous. So then actually being a productive part of the offense this past Sunday was a cool moment to see the development."

It's also interesting that during Aaron Rodgers's now infamous rant on the Pat McAfee show on Wednesday which may have gotten him cut by the Steelers, Rodgers mentioned Mendoza and praised him for his play against Denver.

Rodgers may have his character flaws that have had three teams get rid of him in recent years. But there's no questioning he is one of the greatest to ever spin it in the NFL. Hare to understate how impressive it is to be compared so directly to that caliber of QB in just your second NFL start.

Now the Raiders just need to develop more of a run threat to allow more opportunities for Mendoza to show it off.

#2927. S&P 500 Sell-Off: Here's Why I'm Not Panicking -- and What I'm Doing Instead

History shows why it's important to stay invested.

By Dave Kovaleski – Nov 26, 2026 at 10:45AM EST

Monday was a great example of why investors shouldn't panic sell during a correction or significant market downturn. On Nov. 23, the S&P 500 jumped nearly 5%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average surged 3% and the Nasdaq Composite had one of its best days ever, rising 1,471 points, or 6.5%.

As of Nov. 20, the Nasdaq had been in near bear market territory, down 18% from its all-time high of 27,599 on Oct. 6. But on Nov. 23, with its nearly 1,500-point gain, it surged to 24,089. It is still down 12% from Oct. 6, and there may be volatile days ahead, but days like Monday are one reason you don't want to panic sell during a downturn.

If you did sell shares in a Nasdaq or tech-focused ETF, or a stock like Nvidia, which gained some 30% on the day alone after CEO Jensen Huang was rehired, you would have lost out on a huge return.

And it's just not a one-off type scenario that won't ever happen again. There are stats to show how much it can negatively impact your portfolio when you miss the market's best days during the correction of a bear market.

Image source: OpenAI (AI-generated illustration).

Earlier this year, the Hartford Funds released a report that examined this very subject. It found that from 1950 through 2025, 76% of the market's best days were either during a bear market or within the first two months of a bull market. Of that, 48% came during a bear market. We're not yet in a bear market, but that could certainly change in the months ahead.

The Hartford report also compiled some numbers on how missing out on the best days would affect a long-term portfolio. It revealed that $10,000 invested in the S&P 500 in 1996 would grow to $192,167 after 30 years, through 2025, based on the average annual total returns of the S&P 500.

If you missed the market's 10 best days, that would bring it down to $85,490. If you missed the best 20 days, the total would be $49,551. If you missed the market's best 30 days, you'd have $31,123 -- 84% less than if you stayed fully invested all 30 years.

Fidelity did a similar study in August, putting a slightly finer point on it. It looks at the impact of missing the market's best days since 1988. A $10,000 investment in the S&P 500 from Jan. 1, 1988 to Dec. 31, 2025 would generate $616,013. Missing just the five best days would result in 38% less in your portfolio, about $380,479.

There are a few things to understand that should help guide you through bear markets and rough patches.

One, understand that bear markets do not last as long as bull markets, nor do they have a bigger net effect on your portfolio. A study by the Hartford Funds also found that there have been 27 bear markets since 1928 and 28 bull markets. The average length of a bear market is 289 days, while the average bull market lasts 988 days. Further, the Hartford Funds research showed that stocks lose 35% on average in a bear market and gain 112% on average in a bull market.

Two, over the past 30 years, the S&P 500 has averaged a 14.4% annualized total return, while the Nasdaq has averaged a 17.2% return. That's through the dot-com bust, the Great Recession, the COVID bear market, and the 2022 bear market. So, patience has been rewarded over the years.

And three, downturns and bear markets are a great time to buy, not sell. Many good companies with strong fundamentals in market-leading positions get caught up in negative waves. When that happens, it is time to buy these great stocks at discounts.

In general, downturns are an opportune time to review your holdings. While there may be great buys out there in a bear market, you may also see some stocks that are still overvalued, may be overleveraged, and may be facing industry or economic headwinds.

The best thing to do is check their P/E ratios and valuation metrics, as well as their earnings outlooks. You may find the money invested in those stocks is put to better use in a broad ETF that tracks the Nasdaq or S&P 500 or one of the discount stocks you identified. This is how I approach downturns.

#2925. Meta Platforms Just Partnered With Walmart. Here's Why Investors Should Pay Attention

Meta landing Walmart as a shopping partner may signal that the tech leader's AI spending could eventually pay off.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 26, 2026 at 11:05AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Meta Platforms ›

One important debate between Meta Platforms' (META +3.40%) bulls and bears is whether the company's massive artificial intelligence (AI) spending is justified. The bears certainly have a point: The tech leader's free cash flow is declining, largely due to capex spending tied to its AI build-out. In the third quarter, Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.1 billion, compared with positive free cash flow of $10.6 billion in the year-ago quarter.

That's a significant decline. However, Meta Platforms' AI-related initiatives are slowly taking shape, and a recent partnership with Walmart (WMT -0.28%) highlights the opportunity ahead for the company.

First, let's back up a little. Meta Platforms released its Muse AI agent to the public in early September. It quickly became one of the most downloaded iOS apps. Meta later released several other versions of Muse, including one for small businesses. Management noted on the company's third-quarter earnings call that 10 million people in the U.S. and Canada had set up Muse within a month and a half of its release. The tech giant also said that 25% of those who had set up Muse used it daily, and growth remained strong.

But how does Meta plan to monetize Muse? The AI agent offers subscriptions for people who want higher usage capabilities (the base version is free). But Meta is targeting a potentially more lucrative opportunity. It plans to charge retailers transaction fees every time Muse helps a consumer make a purchase. That brings us to the company's partnership with Walmart. Meta Platforms and Walmart have partnered to integrate Muse with Walmart's e-commerce infrastructure through the retailer's Agent Gateway.

The integration allows consumers to use Muse to discover products, compare prices, find deals, and complete purchases on their behalf, subject to user-defined permissions and spending limits. While the precise financial details of this deal were not disclosed, Meta could earn commission fees for transactions Muse helps facilitate.

Walmart is one of the largest retailers in the world, so this partnership is an important one for Meta. It could also help increase Muse's adoption. Some retailers are hesitant to get on board because they fear it will disrupt their business models and undermine their ability to protect customer relationships, as Meta's CFO, Susan Li, said.

This may happen if agents like Muse become the primary shopping interface -- instead of visiting a retailer's website, consumers could ask an agent to compare prices, quality, and other factors across many retailers, thereby potentially reducing retailers' direct relationships with consumers. Even so, consumers' adoption will largely determine where this market is headed.

If Muse can continue gaining users, a significant percentage of them use the agent to make purchases, and at least some retailers start adapting to the change -- as Walmart is doing -- that could force other retailers to follow suit. Eventually, Meta could generate significant sales from transaction fees.

Of course, Meta's core advertising business remains healthy. In the third quarter, the company's revenue increased by 26% year over year to $64.6 billion. It also ended the period with 3.64 billion daily active people, an increase of 3% year over year. Meta's AI-powered algorithms across its websites and apps have helped grow engagement.

As CEO Mark Zuckerberg pointed out, Facebook and Instagram are hitting near all-time highs in engagement, and Threads -- Meta's competitor to X (formerly Twitter) -- has overtaken X in monthly actives. The point is that even before we look at Meta's opportunity with Muse, the company's underlying business looks strong. It's also worth noting that building the capacity required to support an agent like Muse requires substantial upfront investments.

Meta is dishing out that money today, but the company's expenses in this area should decrease over time, and as Muse gains traction, revenue growth, profits, margins, and free cash flow could jump. Lastly, Meta is trading at 16.3x forward earnings, which, in my view, is more than fair, considering the company's robust underlying business and long-term opportunities in advertising and AI.

This team makes much more sense on paper than it does on the court. The Cavs have more well-rounded role players than they have had in recent years. This has shown through when they get opportunities to shine. Yet, that may not be what works with this particular core.

Somehow, the Cavs have constructed a team that relies heavily on their guards to be at their best to win alongside a supporting cast that doesn't really fit with how the guards play. But that style isn't conducive to a team that has struggled to knock down catch-and-shoot threes (36.3%, 16th in the league), doesn't have a versatile defense, and features two bigs that need to be force-fed to be effective offensively.

Koby Altman has built an interesting supporting cast. On paper, it would fit nicely with a team that played like the 2024-25 Cavs. However, this isn't that team. Any hopes of playing that way went out the window when they traded for Harden last winter.

Cleveland has been one of the most inconsistent groups this season. Few teams have as impressive highs and as disappointing lows. Many factors contribute to that, but one of them is that the puzzle pieces don't really fit together. Or at the very least, they don't fit with how they're being used now.

There's no easy fix for this. Getting a team to drastically change how they play 19 games into the season is nearly impossible. At the same time, there isn't an easy way to pivot off either the star or supporting players due to their contracts. It's difficult to see a trade drastically changing the outlook of this team. They're more or less committed to this current group and playstyle. And I'm just not sure what the pathway is to being a legit title contender, which is what you would hope they'd be coming off a run to the Eastern Conference Finals.

Even when the stars are at or near their best, this team isn't.

#2920. Eli Lilly Is Now Worth 7 Novo Nordisks. Here’s How the Gap Got So Wide.

Eli Lilly's GLP-1 weight-loss drugs continue to grow at a rapid clip.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 26, 2026 at 11:20AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Eli Lilly ›

Eli Lilly's (LLY -1.09%) market cap is roughly $1.2 trillion as of this writing. Novo Nordisk's (NVO +1.64%) market cap is $172.5 billion. Both are very large companies, but Eli Lilly is roughly seven times as large as Novo Nordisk. All the same, they are the number one and two players in the GLP-1 weight-loss niche of the pharmaceutical sector.

Given that Novo Nordisk was the first to market with its GLP-1 shot, how did Eli Lilly get so much bigger? The story is a mix of self-inflicted wounds, intense competition, and short-term investors on Wall Street who may be missing a long-term investment opportunity. Here's what you need to know.

Novo Nordisk brought out its Wegoy/Ozempic GLP-1 weight-loss shot before Eli Lilly introduced Mounjaro/Zepbound. That should have given Novo Nordisk a big head start, but the company underestimated the intensity of demand. It couldn't produce enough of the drugs, which opened the market to generic competition more quickly than usual. When Eli Lilly introduced its own GLP-1 drug, Novo Nordisk was competing with both Mounjaro/Zepbound and generic versions of its own drugs at the same time. Adding to the problem was the fact that Mounjaro/Zepbound was more effective. It isn't hard to see why Eli Lilly quickly took the lead in this emerging drug niche.

Eli Lilly hasn't looked back. Through the first nine months of 2026, Mounjaro revenues grew 89%, while Zepbound revenues increased by 56%. The two drugs now account for 65% of the company's revenues. To be fair, that's a concentration problem, given that the patents protecting the two drugs will eventually expire.

However, investors have taken heart in the fact that Eli Lilly is using the cash influx from its GLP-1 drugs to invest in its pipeline. Notably, management has specifically been broadening the drug maker's portfolio into new treatment areas to create a more diversified business. Given the still-strong growth in the GLP-1 niche and the company's expanding drug portfolio, investors have only gotten more and more upbeat about Eli Lilly's future.

Novo Nordisk is definitely working from behind right now, attempting to catch up to the GLP-1 leader. And given the depressed stock price and lofty 4.5% yield, Wall Street doesn't seem convinced that it can make a comeback. And yet, Novo Nordisk was again first to market with its Wegovy GLP-1 pill, beating out Eli Lilly's Foundayo. Foundayo is not the same drug as Mounjaro/Zepbound, so it hasn't performed quite as well as the Wegovy pill. In fact, the Wegovy pill has seen even stronger demand than Novo Nordisk's GLP-1 shots. This time, however, Novo Nordisk is making sure it can adequately supply the market.

There's just one small problem. Despite the success of the pill, the company's financial results have been weak as it shifts to a low-price, high-volume model. Novo Nordisk appears to be making strong progress, given that it increased both its revenue and earnings guidance in the third quarter of 2026. But Wall Street is clearly in a show-me mood.

If you are a long-term dividend investor, however, the stock could be worth a look. Its lofty 4.5% yield is supported by a very strong 33% payout ratio. (For reference, Eli Lilly's yield is a tiny 0.5%.) And Novo Nordisk is clearly still an innovative drugmaker, given its head start with both a GLP-1 shot and a pill.

If you want to buy an industry leader, Eli Lilly is clearly a company you should look at in the pharmaceutical sector. However, the average pharma stock's price-to-earnings ratio is roughly 27x, and Eli Lilly's is 44x. Meanwhile, Novo Nordisk's P/E is just under 10x. If valuation matters to you, Novo Nordisk may be more attractive, and even if it isn't, Eli Lilly could still be too expensive for your tastes.

#2912. Stock-Split Watch: 3 Industrial Stocks That Could Split in 2027

These three high-priced industrial stocks could be credible 2027 split candidates.

By Todd Shriber – Nov 26, 2026 at 11:45AM EST

10 stocks we like better than W.W. Grainger ›

Some investors, particularly those new to the game, like the idea of forward stock splits (the good kind of split) because they're getting more shares of the stock in question.

In reality, forward stock splits don't alter a company's overall market value. That corporate action merely lowers the share price to make it more accommodating to a broader swath of market participants.

As nice as it is when companies do that, the frequency with which corporations are engaging in forward splits has been declining since the early 1980s. Blame the advent of fractional shares and the increased catering to institutional investors, who aren't price-sensitive.

Stock splits aren’t a sure thing, but these three industrial stocks could make the move in 2027. Image source: Getty Images.

In the first half of this year, fewer than 40 companies engaged in forward share splits. But hope always burns on this front, and there's nothing like four-figure or high triple-digit price tags to stoke the flames of a stock split.

So, with that in mind, Deere (DE +4.44%), Transdigm Group (TDG +1.90%), and W.W. Grainger (GWW +1.28%) are three industrial stocks that may be primed for share splits in 2027.

Among credible split candidates, W.W. Grainger isn't the most glamorous or most popular name, but it warrants a place in the split conversation. For example, the stock's Monday, Nov. 23 closing price of $1,390.12 is the second-highest among all industrial stocks, trailing only conglomerate Seaboard.

Likely more instructive than that is the fact that Grainger has a history of splitting. It has undergone five 2-for-1 splits since 1970. The rub is that the last one occurred in June 1998, suggesting this industrial parts supplier may not be prioritizing share splits and that 2027 may come and go without one.

With or without a move to lower its share price to woo more retail investors, Grainger offers other sources of appeal. Those include wide-moat positioning in the industrial and factory products supply space and its status as a Dividend King, or one of the companies that have raised dividends for at least 50 consecutive years.

Like Grainger, Deere, one of the largest industrial companies, at least has a history of stock splits. The agricultural machinery giant has done so four times since 1972, though its last split occurred in December 2007.

Deere's number of splits is comparable to that of other industrial companies. Likewise, these moves are a reminder of the value of long-term investing because market participants who bought just one share of Deere before the September 1972 split would have 24 shares today.

To be sure, there is no fixed price at which a company considers or is obligated to split its shares. But with a Monday, Nov. 23 closing print of $633.03, Deere is merely the 11th-most expensive industrial stock in dollar terms.

That doesn't mean Deere won't announce a forward split next year. The company may look to broaden its investor base, boost liquidity, or signal confidence in its growth projections. Still, ultimately, split hunters need to consider Deere a wait-and-see proposition.

Just as there is no fixed price at which companies are more likely to mull splits, there is no such thing as an "overdue" split, and that wisdom is worth remembering as it relates to Transdigm. The purveyor of components and parts found on nearly every commercial and military aircraft worldwide, Transdigm celebrated its 20th anniversary as a public company in March. Over those two-plus decades, it's never split its shares.

That brings us to today, well, Monday, Nov. 23, when the stock closed at $1,158.65 -- a price exceeded by just three other industrial stocks. Given its lack of split history, it's possible that Transdigm isn't actively evaluating a split to make its share price more accommodating.

On the other hand, splits have to start somewhere, but that thesis is rooted in a lot of hope and not much else.

Looking ahead to 2027, if investors are going to bet on anything with Transdigm, the safe wager is that the company will remain acquisitive. Estimates vary, but Transdigm has made dozens upon dozens of acquisitions since its founding in 1993, and CEO Michael Lisman recently said the company has "substantial M&A firepower...in excess of $10 billion."

#2909. Reddit's 2027 Outlook: Scaling Advertising Business Drives Sustained Growth

This under-the-radar communication stock boasts a Superscore of 86 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 26, 2026 at 11:51AM EST

Picture a digital town square where millions of people gather daily to argue about everything from stock picks to the best way to boil an egg. That square is Reddit (RDDT +1.29%), an internet content platform that has successfully converted years of chaotic, human-generated chatter into a highly profitable advertising business. As of November 19, 2026, the stock trades at $91.04, reflecting a wild ride since its public debut, with the stock falling 52% over the last year despite significant operational progress.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns Reddit an overall Superscore of 86 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39).

An 86 Superscore places Reddit in the Top ~3% of every company we score, ahead of roughly 97 out of every 100 companies we track. This score serves as a data-driven starting point; the following breakdown weighs the company's recent operational surge against the significant risks that remain, allowing you to decide if it earns a place in your portfolio.

Reddit runs a highly capital-efficient business, earning significant profits on a small base of hard physical assets. This means that each dollar of revenue growth effectively translates into outsized returns, a trait that often leads the market to apply a higher valuation multiple, though this efficiency does not fully eliminate the risks posed by shifting search traffic and platform competition.

The Hidden Gems Superscore reflects The Motley Fool's proprietary AI-driven evaluation of a company across product, financial, leadership, and valuation pillars as of the article date and may change over time. Performance figures are point-in-time. Past performance does not guarantee future results.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is only one data-driven signal, and it should be weighed alongside your own independent research, financial goals, and personal risk tolerance before making any decisions.

I'm a big fan of Reddit, both the community-based platform and the company, which is why I bought the stock. Its valuable discussion forums in the era of synthetic content generated by artificial intelligence can easily enable Reddit to achieve years of ongoing business expansion.

The one key headwind is how AI is transforming the discoverability of Reddit's site. The company has seen a meaningful decline in consumer traffic from search engines to the point where its Q3 DAUqs of 130.3 million could drop in Q4. Reddit is addressing this challenge by targeting logged-in users. These DAUqs contribute more than 80% of revenue, so management is focused on growing this segment.

One way Reddit is protecting its content from AI bot-scrapping is by requiring visitors to log in. This not only protects community data, but also improves the targeting for ads, which should improve the revenue Reddit generates.

The company is also making improvements to its recommendation engine, used to personalize content and improve customer retention. As a result, management reported that new users who returned to Reddit are up 50% year over year in Q3, indicating its efforts are working.

The transition away from a dependence on search engines will take time, and in the interim, its shares may experience higher volatility. But the success it's delivering is why I believe Reddit's business will continue to strengthen over the next several years, and its stock price with it.

#2907. Should You Buy Nvidia After Its Sharp Decline? Warren Buffett’s Long-Held Investment Philosophy Provides a Clear Answer

Is it time to get greedy while others are fearful?

By Adam Levy – Nov 26, 2026 at 12:15PM EST

The past six weeks have been tough for Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) and its shareholders. In October, growing concerns about how businesses will finance future build-outs for artificial intelligence (AI) started weighing on semiconductor stocks.

In early November, the mid-term election results showed a clear rebuke of massive data centers and the potential for increased regulations to slow development. And, of course, Jensen Huang's affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and two employees ultimately led to his temporary resignation, leaving Nvidia without a permanent CEO for 11 days.

The end result is a stock well off its all-time high, despite having just reported third-quarter revenue and earnings growth that both exceeded 100%. CFO and interim-CEO Colette Kress said it's "business as usual" at Nvidia, painting an optimistic picture on the company's earnings call. So, with the stock trading at a level rarely seen in the past year, should investors consider buying the stock? Warren Buffett's long-held investment philosophy can provide the answer to that question.

Warren Buffett. Image source: Getty Images.

Many investors might look at Nvidia's drop and think, "The market is fearful; I should get greedy." After all, Kress just increased her guidance for fiscal 2028 revenue growth from 70% to 75%. Wall Street analysts see Nvidia's earnings per share climbing above $16 next year. The stock trades at ridiculously low multiples.

While it's true that Buffett has long noted that one of the keys to his success as an investor is "to be greedy only when others are fearful," that's not the only requirement for a good investment. Buffett also only invested in stocks when he had a high degree of confidence in their future. If a company was deemed too difficult to understand or forecast, he stayed away.

"Investors should remember that their scorecard is not computed using Olympic-diving methods: Degree-of-difficulty doesn't count," Buffett wrote in his 1994 letter to Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +0.54%) (BRKB +0.37%) shareholders. "If you are right about a business whose value is largely dependent on a single key factor that is both easy to understand and enduring, the payoff is the same as if you had correctly analyzed an investment alternative characterized by many constantly shifting and complex variables."

At this point, Nvidia's business is more like the latter than the former. Huang's actions and the potential repercussions leave many unanswered questions for investors. The high level of uncertainty about Nvidia's future explains why it trades at such a low multiple. It's not fear; it's a rational response to the recent events impacting the company.

There are a couple of alternatives to Nvidia stock trading at earnings multiples that aren't much higher than the chip leader.

Buffett himself initiated a position in Alphabet (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%) in 2025. His thesis was based on the company's ability to deploy huge sums of capital with a high return on investment. Specifically, Buffett was talking about its Google Cloud Platform and its data centers full of GPUs and custom silicon.

Alphabet is well-positioned to deliver high returns on invested capital in Google Cloud because of its full-stack approach to AI. It designs the systems and chips in its data centers; it offers a full cloud platform for developers (not just infrastructure); it develops a frontier large language model; and it integrates AI capabilities across its products.

Google Cloud CEO Thomas Kurian said that its payback period for the average server in its data centers is just two years, and its custom Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) systems break even within half that time. With a useful life of six years and a good operating margin, that's an exceptional return on invested capital. That return on invested capital is set to climb as more spending shifts from data center shells to revenue-generating servers.

Another company positioned to grow based on a single key factor is Broadcom (AVGO -0.07%). Broadcom is the leading custom AI accelerator design partner, the one Alphabet uses for most of its TPUs. Growing demand for custom silicon, which costs less up front than GPUs and offers better price-performance, should result in its AI chip revenue climbing faster than the competition's for years to come. Its lead is protected by its intellectual property and its close relationship with its foundry partner, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing.

While Nvidia currently trades around 16 times earnings expectations, Broadcom's stock price isn't much higher at 17 times forward earnings. Alphabet is more expensive at 21 times forward earnings, but it's arguably the least risky of the group, considering its digital advertising business remains a huge cash cow funding its growth.

#2904. Alphabet Could Be the Safest Way to Own Anthropic. Here’s the Math.

Alphabet owns a piece of Anthropic and also gets paid to supply its computing power, which is a nice way to be on both sides of the AI boom.

By Patrick Sanders – Nov 26, 2026 at 12:27PM EST

It didn't take long for some of the early enthusiasm surrounding Anthropic (ANT +1.08%) stock to wear off. Shares of the artificial intelligence (AI) company opened at $111.50 on their first day of trading on Nov. 19, but fell below their initial public offering (IPO) price of $83 on just the fourth day of trading.

I'm not surprised. Tech IPOs have a history of volatility, and it's particularly difficult to put a valuation on a company growing as quickly as Anthropic.

But there's another way to invest in the company that developed Claude. And the more I look at the numbers, the more I like the idea of buying Alphabet (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%) stock instead.

Alphabet has a sizable ownership stake in Anthropic, and it's also well positioned to earn tens of billions of dollars by leasing computing capacity to the AI lab.

At the time of this writing, Anthropic has a market cap of about $1 trillion. That puts the value of Alphabet's 13.7% ownership stake in it at $137 billion. Every time Anthropic gains $100 billion in market cap, Alphabet's investment increases by $13.7 billion, assuming the tech stalwart doesn’t sell any of its shares.

If Anthropic grows into a $2 trillion company, Alphabet's stake would be worth $274 billion. And if Anthropic becomes a $3 trillion company, Alphabet would be sitting on an investment worth $411 billion.

Alphabet only becomes a stronger, wealthier company as Anthropic grows. That's a nice position to be in. And it doesn't even factor in that Anthropic also will be paying a lot to Google Cloud for its services.

Anthropic's S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission revealed that the company is committed to spending at least $121.3 billion for Google Cloud services between April 2026 and July 2033.

That works out to roughly $16.7 billion annually over the life of the agreement, although the actual payments won't necessarily be spread out evenly over those years.

What's particularly attractive about this agreement from Alphabet's perspective is that Anthropic is obligated to pay for the contracted computing capacity regardless of how much it actually uses. If Anthropic's needs fall short of its commitment, it generally still has to pay Google the difference, subject to certain contractual adjustments and exceptions.

And that's only part of the business relationship.

Anthropic also estimates that it will spend another $125 billion to $150 billion for additional computing capacity powered by Tensor Processing Units (TPUs). They are the custom AI chips that Alphabet designs in conjunction with Broadcom (AVGO -0.07%).

Anthropic can obtain that computing capacity directly from Google or through third-party providers. We don't know how much of that money will end up in Alphabet's coffers, and the payments depend on several factors, including how much capacity is made available.

But Alphabet has an opportunity to do considerably more business with Anthropic than the $121.3 billion commitment suggests.

There's an important distinction between investing in Anthropic and doing business with Anthropic.

The AI company generated $17 billion in revenue in the third quarter, but it has also committed to spending approximately $900.9 billion, nearly all of it related to computing and infrastructure.

Anthropic needs to generate enough revenue from Claude to justify those expenditures. Alphabet, meanwhile, is being paid to provide some of that infrastructure.

And most importantly, Alphabet isn't entirely dependent on Anthropic for its success. It has its own AI platform, Gemini, a fast-growing cloud computing business that serves customers beyond Anthropic, and a highly profitable advertising business built around Google Search.

I think Anthropic could be an outstanding company over the next decade, but I'm much less certain about what its stock will do in the next year or two.

That's why I like Alphabet stock here. Anthropic could be a disappointing investment for its shareholders yet remain an extremely valuable customer for Google Cloud. I don't need to get Anthropic's valuation right to make money by owning Alphabet stock.

#2902. Here's What Would Prove Me Wrong About Netflix Stock

The streaming giant's stock looks cheap to me around $60. Three numbers might change my mind.

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 26, 2026 at 12:41PM EST

This month, I've argued Netflix (NFLX +0.92%) stock is worth buying near $60, mostly because the streaming giant's profits keep rising even as its sales growth cools. The shares ended Wednesday, Nov. 25, at $59.41. That put them just over their Nov. 20 close of $56.73, the lowest since May 2024, and about 56% under their highest close ever, $133.91 on June 30, 2025 (adjusted for last year's 10-for-1 split).

At $59.41, shares sell for around 16 times earnings, using analysts' average estimate for 2027. In comparison, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%) was at 17.1 times forward earnings, according to a Nov. 20 FactSet report.

But what would prove me wrong? Any one of three things would: total view hours (the time members spend watching) down from a year earlier, 2027 ad revenue below $3 billion, or a 2027 operating margin target under 32%. Each would suggest a weaker business than the price assumes.

Much of the streaming service's growth comes from hiking prices, which usually works only while members keep watching. They still are. In the first half of 2026, members watched more than 97 billion hours on Netflix, up 2% year over year and a bit faster than the 1.5% gain in all of 2025.

Members also took this year's price hikes much as they took past ones, management said in October.

But 2% is slow growth, and management knows it. In its third-quarter shareholder letter, Netflix acknowledged "some investor concern about our view hours growth" and said it wants to speed up that growth.

The next test is in the first quarter of 2027, when Netflix plans to change its viewing report from every six months to once a year. If that report shows total view hours lower than a year before, I'd read it as a sign that members are watching less while paying more. That might make future price hikes tougher to push through.

Advertising was supposed to become Netflix's other big growth engine, and it's taking longer than planned. Ad revenue climbed more than 150% in 2025, to over $1.5 billion, and in January, management expected it to roughly double this year. In October, Netflix said it now expects around $2.7 billion instead, because some of its ad features have rolled out a bit more slowly than planned.

Even $2.7 billion would be fast growth, just slower than 2025's rate and the original plan. And Netflix plans to launch its ad-supported tier in 15 more countries in 2027, starting with 12 of them in March.

My bar is the $3 billion Netflix first expected for this year. If 2027 ad revenue can't top that level, even with another year plus 15 new countries, I'd see the issue as more than a slow rollout.

My case leans hardest on Netflix's operating margin. Management expects it to reach 31.5% in 2026, up from 26.7% in 2024, and says that implies operating income growth of over 20% this year. This widening margin is a big reason earnings per share can keep climbing faster than sales.

"[O]ur intent is to grow our operating margin each year," the company wrote in its January shareholder letter, adding that the size of each year's rise will vary.

Even my more cautious forecasts assume the margin improves by half a point a year. That makes Netflix's 2027 margin target, which management says it'll share in January, the figure I'll watch most closely. A target under 32% might mean Netflix must spend more just to keep growing. And it would leave my earnings forecasts for the next few years too high.

Debt isn't on my list. Netflix finished September with net debt (debt minus cash) of around $7.1 billion, under half the operating income it expects this year.

None of the three has happened. View hours are still climbing, ad revenue should still jump this year, and Netflix still expects its operating margin to hit 31.5%.

For now, my case stands, and at roughly $59 I think the growth stock is still worth a look. But if Netflix's fourth-quarter report includes a 2027 margin target below 32% or an ad revenue forecast under $3 billion, I'd need to rethink that.

#2901. Game Recap: Suns run out of fuel in 116-104 loss to Rockets

The Phoenix Suns fought valiantly, but couldn't finish the road trip undefeated as the Houston Rockets held off a late push by the Suns and earned a 116-104 victory. Phoenix clawed within two at 99-97, but the Rockets finished on a 17-7 run.

Alperen Sengun finished the game with 23 points, 11 rebounds and 11 assists, recording his third triple-double of the season. Seven of those rebounds were on the offensive glass. Steven Adams also snatched six offensive boards, and the Rockets won the overall rebounding battle 51-38.

Oso Ighodaro had 13 points on 6-for-8 shooting, but managed just two rebounds in 20 minutes before fouling out. Khaman Maluach added 12 points of his own and made several outstanding plays, but is still a little too raw to compete with the burly Adams and All-Star Sengun.

The Suns were an abysmal 5-for-32 from three-point range in the game. Jordan Goodwin was 2-for-4, which made the rest of the team 3-for-28. That looks like a typo. Posting a -13 on the boards and shooting 15.6% from the three-point range makes it hard to win a basketball game. A bad shooting night is one thing, but the rebounding situation is starting to look like a fatal flaw for this squad.

Miles Bridges struggled through his worst offensive game of the season, recording just two points while shooting 0-6 from three-point range and 1-8 overall. Devin Booker had a game-high 25 points and Jalen Green chipped in 16, but they were 6-18 and 8-21 from the field, respectively. Booker did knock down 11-of-12 free throws to continue his impressive performance from the line this season.

The Suns' bench group played well overall. In addition to Maluach's performance, Goodwin scored 11 points and grabbed five rebounds, but played just 17 minutes. Kevin Durant recorded a double-double with 20 points and 10 rebounds, while Amen Thompson recorded his own with 18 and 10. That is two more than the Suns had.

The Rockets' starters were just too much for Phoenix to contend with tonight. Plain and simple.

The Suns' aspirations of advancing to the next round in the NBA Cup have been quashed with this loss.

Phoenix will try to get back in the win column when they host the Dallas Mavericks and Cooper Flagg at 7:00pm Friday.

The Rockets raced out to an 11-4 lead with Kevin Durant getting five quick points. The Suns recovered to tie it up at 11, and for a long stretch in the quarter it looked like we might be watching a defense-optional game.

KD kept pouring it in and already had 11 points when the Rockets went up 22-18. At that time, the Rockets held a 10-2 free-throw disparity over the Suns.

The Suns were trying to play physical, but the referees didn't let them impose their will, opting to let Durant and Adams flop to get calls. Maluach ended up with a fairly weak flagrant one as he somehow managed to plant Adams face first on the ground. Or maybe Adams just laid down that way on purpose.

Jordan Goodwin entered and hit a quick three-pointer to pull the Suns within one at 23-22. Goodwin is shooting a career-high 38.6% this season from three-point range.

A dunk and damage for a three-point play from Maluach tied the game at 25. Khaman was playing very well overall but still displayed hand problems during the game. Goodwin hit his second three-pointer to give the Suns a 32-27 lead courtesy of a 10-2 run. The first quarter ended 33-29 in the Suns' favor. Booker finished the quarter with 13 points and survived a massive block by Amen Thompson.

Goodwin was fouled on a three-point attempt after making his first two. He sank all three free throws to give the Suns their biggest lead of the game at 38-31. The Oso/Kennard/Gillespie/Brooks/Goodwin lineup meshed well together, but the Rockets started punishing them inside.

Steven Adams grabbed four quick offensive rebounds. There just isn't a player on the Suns with the size and strength of Adams.

He helped the Rockets go on a 13-7 run to cut the Suns' lead to 45-44. Maluach was still making his presence felt. A few plays after a block on Sengun, he nailed a jumper as the shot clock expired. In between that, he mishandled the ball on offense for a turnover. Such are the highs and lows with the 20-year-old.

Sengun was still winning the matchup overall. After another offensive rebound, Sengun hit a three to cap an 11-2 run that put Houston up 57-51. That turned into a 20-7 run by the time the quarter was over, and Houston led 66-56 at the half. The Suns had squandered their early lead and would trail the remainder of the game.

Booker finished the first half with 15 points and four assists, while Goodwin chipped in nine points.

The Rockets starters had a well-balanced attack. Durant scored 14, Sengun had 13 along with seven rebounds and six assists, Jabari Smith Jr. poured in 12, and Amen just missed double figures with nine points. Houston led the battle of the boards 27-15, including 12-4 on the offensive end. The Rockets shot 26 free throws, but made just 14, while the Suns were 11-14 from the charity stripe.

#2899. Nvidia Stock Has Found Its Next Big Catalyst, Making the Stock a Once-in-a-Decade Buying Opportunity.

Nvidia's physical AI revenue is growing at an incredible pace.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 26, 2026 at 12:57PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) has been hammered on the market lately. Even a strong set of quarterly results from the company earlier this month couldn't change investor sentiment, before the reinstatement of co-founder Jensen Huang as CEO sparked a massive rally earlier this week.

However, investors will do well to look past the noise and focus on the company's financial performance. Nvidia easily beat Wall Street's expectations for the third quarter of fiscal 2027 (which ended Oct. 25). The company's guidance clearly indicates that its incredible growth is sustainable, driven by the data center business that played a central role in boosting its revenue and earnings last quarter.

However, there is another segment that's starting to move the needle for the company, and I won't be surprised to see this business becoming massive over the next decade.

Data centers have been at the forefront of the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution so far. However, the technology is now moving from data centers into the real world. Physical AI -- the integration of AI into machines and physical objects such as cars, drones, robots, factory equipment, medical equipment, and other devices -- is emerging as the next big growth driver for Nvidia.

Nvidia notes that its physical AI revenue almost tripled year over year in the previous quarter. What's more, Nvidia expects to generate $25 billion in physical AI revenue in fiscal 2027. That's a huge leap over fiscal 2026, when physical AI added $6 billion to Nvidia's top line. The company is on track to clock almost $424 billion in revenue for fiscal 2027, meaning physical AI will account for nearly 6% of its top line this year.

Nvidia's incredible growth in physical AI isn't surprising, as it has been pushing the envelope in this space. Companies such as Caterpillar, Boston Dynamics, LG Electronics, and other robotics companies are using Nvidia's robotics solutions to develop AI-enabled robots. The semiconductor giant also offers a platform to help customers develop and deploy humanoid robots in healthcare, manufacturing, and retail.

It won't be surprising to see Nvidia's physical AI business become significantly bigger over the coming decade. Counterpoint Research estimates that cumulative physical AI shipments could reach 145 million units by 2035. Barclays estimates that the physical AI market could be worth $1.4 trillion in 2035, which means that Nvidia is scratching the surface of a massive growth opportunity.

As such, there is a solid chance that Nvidia's physical AI revenue will grow at a solid clip over the next 10 years, complementing the growth of the company's data center business.

Nvidia stock is down 26% over the past month. As a result, investors can now buy it at just 16.7 times earnings. The forward earnings multiple of 9.3 is even more attractive for a company whose earnings are poised to double this year. Additionally, Nvidia has raised its fiscal 2028 revenue growth estimate by five percentage points to 75%.

Analysts have raised their earnings per share (EPS) expectations for the next couple of years, which isn't surprising considering that the company is already sitting on a lucrative multi-year growth opportunity in AI data center infrastructure.

NVDA EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

Nvidia noted on the latest earnings call that the cloud industry's backlog now sits at a whopping $2.5 trillion, up from $2 trillion just three months ago. This huge backlog will ensure that hyperscalers and AI companies continue to invest in more chips, and that's great news for Nvidia considering that it controls 75% of the AI chip market.

The emergence of additional catalysts, such as physical AI, could help Nvidia consistently beat Wall Street's expectations, improving investor confidence in the stock. So, even though Nvidia has taken a beating lately, its growth prospects are still solid. That's why opportunistic investors may consider capitalizing on this AI stock’s recent slide before it becomes a long-term winner.

#2898. Raiders QB Fernando Mendoza thankful for Aaron Rodgers on Thanksgiving

Somewhere in the middle of airing his grievances with the Pittsburgh Steelers on Wednesday, Aaron Rodgers took a moment to say something nice about a fellow quarterback.

Imagine Fernando Mendoza's surprise to find out it was him.

Rodgers was on "The Pat McAfee Show" explaining how things had gone sideways with Steelers coach Mike McCarthy when he got around to the Denver film. Pittsburgh plays the Broncos on Friday. The Raiders had just been through Denver on Sunday, which is how a rookie making his second NFL start earned a cameo in somebody else's very public breakup.

Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Fernando Mendoza (15) throws against the Denver Broncos during an NFL football game in Denver, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Jack Dempsey

"And I'll tell you: Hey, the kid Mendoza, man. He was running around, making some plays in the scramble."

Funny thing about that tape. If Rodgers goes back to the third quarter and watches what Mendoza did before throwing a 19-yard touchdown pass to tight end Ian Thomas, he might recognize the move the young quarterback busted out.

After all, Mendoza borrowed it from Rodgers.

The Raiders call it the guitar fake. Watch Mendoza's hands as running back Ashton Jeanty approached Mendoza as if he was going to be handed the ball. The empty hand carries out the handoff while the other keeps the football tucked against his body.

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers gestures at a news conference after an NFL football game in Philadelphia, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Chris Szagola

For a beat, the quarterback looks like a guy playing air guitar. By the time Mendoza brings the ball back into view, Thomas is wide open. The extra point tied a game the Raiders once trailed 17-0.

It makes sense that any young quarterback would pick out elements of a future Hall of Famer like Rodgers and make them their own. Even more so in the case of Mendoza, who played his first two seasons at Cal, Rodgers' alma mater.

Aaron Rodgers' NFL options dwindling after ugly Steelers divorce

Mike McCarthy explains 'ultimatum' behind decision to bench Aaron Rodgers

The troubling response to Aaron Rodgers' bombshell claims against Mike McCarthy

Pat McAfee floored by Aaron Rodgers remarks about 'unhealthy' Mike McCarthy that got him cut

"Yeah, Aaron Rodgers, he also went to Cal, so it's a little funny, but it was something that we've worked on a lot," Mendoza said.

Mendoza spent his last college season at Indiana working primarily from the shotgun, several yards behind the center, with the defense in front of him and the back alongside him.

Get under center in Klint Kubiak's offense, and the view changes in a hurry. Mendoza has to take the snap, turn his back on the defense, meet a runner coming downhill, then get his head around and find his receiver. His feet have to sell the handoff, too.

"So, if you don't have a lot of experience detailing under center handoffs, it's sometimes hard to then develop the fakes," Mendoza said.

In April, by Mendoza's own assessment, his guitar fake "looked horrendous."

Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Fernando Mendoza (15) receives instructions during the fourth quarter against the Denver Broncos in Denver, Colorado, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. Kate Benic/UPI/Shutterstock

"So then actually being a productive part of the offense this past Sunday was a cool moment to see the development," Mendoza said.

Imagine showing him those two clips back then. In one, Denver bites hard enough on his handoff for Thomas to get loose. In the other, the guy he borrowed it from interrupts a beef with his head coach to talk a little ball about the kid from Cal.

#2897. Canadiens Mythbusters: Should The Habs Split Up Their First Line?

With the Montreal Canadiens in the midst of a three-game slide, including a frustrating one-sided loss to the Colorado Avalanche, many fans are starting to lose patience with the team's first line.

Usually a dominant force, the trio of Nick Suzuki, Cole Caufield, and Juraj Slafkovsky has managed only six goals at 5v5 this season, well below its typical offensive output.

As a result, the majority of fans are ready to break up the line, despite its success in recent years. Emotions are running high after the game against the Avalanche, which may have influenced the poll, but when it comes to sports fans, opinions are almost always divided.

In this case, roughly 90% of Canadiens fans are ready to move on, a surprisingly cohesive result.

Do you think the Canadiens should split up their 1st line? (Caufield – Suzuki – Slafkovsky) #GoHabsGo

— Montreal Hockey Now (@MTLhockeynow) November 26, 2026

The first step is to evaluate their underlying numbers at 5v5 compared with last season. We will limit the comparison to the first 26 games of each season.

Believe it or not, Slafkovsky, Caufield, and Suzuki are actually controlling a higher share of shots at 5v5 (CF%) as well as more high-danger scoring chances (HDCF%). On the other hand, their share of expected goals (xGF%) and actual goals (GF%) is down slightly.

That being said, any time a line maintains numbers above 50 percent, it is considered above average relative to the rest of the league. When those numbers approach 60 percent, the line is generally described as elite.

It would therefore be incorrect to suggest they are playing poorly despite the lack of goals. Shooting percentages are fickle, and a downturn in efficiency can make a relatively healthy situation look like a red flag.

The bulk of the criticism this year has been levelled at Slafkovsky. In particular, his lack of powerplay goals stands out like a sore thumb. In the interest of fairness, however, the powerplay is heavily influenced by the system in use, and Slafkovsky is far from the only player struggling to score with the man advantage. To put a finer point on the overall powerplay issues, the Habs are currently running one of the worst powerplays in franchise history.

However, it is also fair to note that Alexandre Texier has produced encouraging underlying numbers at 5v5 in Slafkovsky's place. With Texier on the wing, the line has controlled more shots, more high-danger scoring chances, and more expected goals.

Isolating their individual production shows that Suzuki is scoring more often while providing fewer assists. This leaves him with a similar points-per-60 rate to the one he posted in the first 26 games of the 2025-26 season.

Interestingly, he is shooting more frequently, and both his individual expected goals (ixG/60) and individual high-danger chances (iHDCF/60) are also much higher.

It may sound like the most obvious conclusion possible, but Suzuki is not holding the first line back by any measure.

As for Caufield, he is scoring at the same pace while producing more assists and more points overall. In fact, every individual metric for Caufield sits higher than it did at this point in the schedule last year.

This is going to shock some, but Slafkovsky has been a little more productive this season at 5v5, even though his goal-scoring has almost collapsed. He's shooting with a lower frequency, but his individual high-danger scoring opportunities have gone through a slight uptick.

Admittedly, before delving into the numbers, I shared the same opinion as the fans who responded to the poll. I was genuinely surprised to find that the individual numbers were similar, if not better, while the underlying numbers remained quite strong.

A semi-functional powerplay would go a long way toward easing much of the fans' weariness, as would a little more shooting luck.

However, it is also fair to note that Caufield and Suzuki have essentially been tied at the hip for most of their Canadiens tenure. We have no point of reference for how they perform away from each other.

With the addition of Dylan Larkin to the lineup, head coach Martin St-Louis will finally have the option to split up his first line while still maintaining a solid top six.

Simply put, there is very little evidence to suggest the Canadiens must break up their first line. At the same time, we should remain open to the possibility that different configurations could produce better results, even if that outcome is statistically unlikely.

All Montreal Canadiens statistics are 5v5 unless otherwise noted, via Natural Stat Trick.

Minnesota Vikings May Be Without Two Key Starters for Sunday's Game
