#2895. This Oversold Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stock Will Skyrocket 123% in 2027, According to Wall Street

DigitalOcean's improving growth trajectory should translate into a big jump in the stock price next year.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 26, 2026 at 1:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than DigitalOcean ›

Cloud infrastructure stocks have been hammered lately as investors have been rotating out of this sector for multiple reasons.

Inflationary conditions led the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates in September, and it could make a similar move in December. A high-interest rate scenario doesn't bode well for companies investing heavily in artificial intelligence (AI) data center infrastructure, as they will have to contend with higher borrowing costs.

This explains why investors have been booking profits in AI infrastructure stocks. DigitalOcean (DOCN -3.09%) has also borne the brunt of the broader sell-off in cloud stocks, with shares of the company losing 55% of their value over the past six months. However, a closer look at DigitalOcean's growth and prospects indicates that the sell-off may be overdone.

DigitalOcean offers on-demand cloud infrastructure services to small and medium-sized companies, developers, and start-ups. DigitalOcean's customers can rent computing resources to run AI workloads in the cloud without investing in expensive infrastructure of their own. The company also offers platform-as-a-service (PaaS) solutions, enabling users to build, customize, and deploy AI applications.

So, DigitalOcean provides a full-stack cloud infrastructure platform that integrates hardware and software. Customers can build AI agents, run business analytics, detect fraud, create chatbots, summarize documents, and perform other tasks using its AI-native cloud platform. The good part is that DigitalOcean's AI services are in solid demand.

The company released its third-quarter results earlier this month, and it became clear that AI is a key catalyst. Its Q3 revenue increased by 40% year over year to $321 million. The company's annual run rate revenue (ARR) also increased by a similar magnitude to $1.28 billion. However, what's worth noting is that DigitalOcean's ARR from AI customers increased by 288% year over year to $376 million.

The robust adoption of DigitalOcean's AI services explains why it expects its revenue growth rate to ramp up next year. DigitalOcean expects its revenue growth to accelerate to 45% year over year in the current quarter. Even better, it sees a 60% jump in revenue next year, which is well above its prior guidance of 50% growth.

Another important point worth noting is that DigitalOcean's software stack is boosting its ARR. I already pointed out that DigitalOcean doesn't make money by just renting out hardware. The company points out that customers are using its software solutions to build and deploy agentic AI applications, which is driving up DigitalOcean's revenue per megawatt (MW) of cloud computing capacity it deploys.

DigitalOcean is on track to end 2026 with 75 MW of active data center capacity. That number is poised to double next year, and it aims to further increase its capacity in 2028. This explains why analysts expect DigitalOcean to clock solid revenue growth in 2028 as well.

DOCN Revenue Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

Importantly, the increasing adoption of its software offerings is poised to drive significant acceleration in earnings per share (EPS) growth.

DOCN EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

The impressive revenue and earnings growth that DigitalOcean is poised to deliver is likely to send its shares soaring, according to Wall Street.

DigitalOcean's median 12-month price target of $160 implies potential upside of 123%, according to estimates compiled by CNN. It is worth noting that 16 of the 18 analysts covering DigitalOcean rate the stock as a buy.

It is easy to see why analysts are bullish about its stock price trajectory over the coming year. DigitalOcean's growth rate is picking up, and it trades at an attractive 8 times sales right now. The U.S. technology sector has an average sales multiple of 5.6, and the slight premium that DigitalOcean enjoys is attributable to its improving growth profile.

I won't be surprised to see DigitalOcean trading at a slightly higher sales multiple after a year, driven by a stronger top-line jump. Assuming it trades at 10 times sales, and its top line reaches $1.96 billion in 2027, the company's market cap could increase to $19.6 billion. That's 130% higher than the current market cap, suggesting that DigitalOcean indeed has the potential to live up to Wall Street's expectations and skyrocket next year.

#2893. Better Defense Stock to Own in 2027: Lockheed Martin vs. L3Harris Technologies

Lockheed Martin and L3Harris Technologies are defense contractors with huge backlogs.

By James Halley – Nov 26, 2026 at 1:32PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Lockheed Martin ›

Lockheed Martin (LMT +2.15%) and L3Harris Technologies (LHX +1.48%) are top defense stocks. Each stock has its advantages. Lockheed is a more stable stock with lower price volatility and a higher-yielding dividend. L3Harris, on the other hand, is showing more growth and benefits from higher margins.

Lockheed Martin's shares have soared this year, up more than 18% as I write this, while L3Harris' have slumped by more than 13%.

Here's a quick comparison of these two defense stocks.

F-35 Stealth fighter jet. Image source: Lockheed Martin.

Lockheed Martin is a much larger company, with nearly three times L3Harris' market cap and employee count. The company's third-quarter backlog of $292 billion provides around 3.6 years of revenue visibility, substantially deeper than L3Harris' 2.25 years for its $53.2 billion backlog.

Lockheed management is predicting high single-digit growth across all four of its business segments in 2027, led by F-35 sustainment/production and a $52.5 billion multiyear award for PAC-3 MSE interceptor missiles. That scale allows the company to thrive even if one segment falters.

As a specialist in defense electronics, resilient communications, and propulsion, L3Harris generated a 15.9% segment operating margin in the third quarter, roughly 500 basis points higher than Lockheed Martin. L3Harris reported third-quarter earnings per share (EPS) of $3.28, up 33% year over year, prompting management to raise full-year EPS guidance to a range of $12.25 to $12.35, an increase of 44% at the midpoint.

Lockheed, on the other hand, saw its third-quarter EPS slip by 11% to $6.44. It reaffirmed full-year EPS of between $29.35 and $30.25, a 38% gain at the midpoint.

Both companies have above-average dividend yields. At their current share prices, Lockheed's dividend yield is 2.42% while L3Harris' is 1.94%. Lockheed has increased its dividend for 24 consecutive years, while L3Harris has raised its dividend for 25 consecutive years. L3Harris has been more aggressive in increasing its dividend over the past decade, raising it by more than 135%, compared to just more than 89% for Lockheed, which could be an advantage in a stock-to-stock matchup.

Despite its share gain this year, Lockheed's stock is trading at a better price point. Its forward price-to-earnings ratio is slightly more than 18 times earnings estimates compared to more than 20 for L3Harris.

Lockheed's biggest problem is that it is the lead on massive hardware programs, such as the F-35, F-16, C-13, and Sikorsky helicopters, and, as such, has lower operating margins, around 10.8% as of the third quarter. It is also affected more by fixed-price development or production charges. It stands to lose more, too, if a Democratic-controlled Congress tightens defense spending.

L3Harris carries more debt, with a debt-to-earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) ratio of 3.1, compared to 1.9 for Lockheed, and its debt payments could easily weigh on its earnings growth. L3Harris carries a heavier debt load relative to its size, with $9 billion in net long-term debt. Much of that is due to the company's $4.7 billion acquisition of Aerojet Rocketdyne in 2023.

However, L3Harris plans to sell a majority stake in its space propulsion and power Systems business to AE Industrial for $845 million, while retaining a 40% ownership stake in the newly created Rocketdyne venture, which includes manufacturing and selling RS-25 rocket engines.

Deciding which stock is best really comes down to your investing needs. Lockheed certainly offers more stability, though with less growth potential.

L3Harris is expected to see more revenue and earnings growth, though its higher valuation shows some of that expected growth is already priced into the stock. If I had to choose, Lockheed is the safer pick because of its scale, better dividend, and lower valuation.

#2888. Caterpillar's $78 Billion Backlog, Explained

Caterpillar's order backlog nearly doubled over the past year, yet shares are trading at just 16.5 times forward earnings. See how this $78 billion unfilled order list sets up the industrial giant for long-term sales growth.

By Anders Bylund – Nov 26, 2026 at 1:37PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Caterpillar ›

Looking at Caterpillar's (CAT +0.57%) stock chart lately feels like a market paradox.

On one hand, the heavy-machinery giant is sitting on a staggering, all-time-high $78 billion order backlog -- nearly double the $39.8 billion list it had a year ago. On the other hand, the stock has been cut roughly in half from its 52-week high, leaving shares trading at a modest 16.5 times forward earnings.

So, what gives? First, that $78 billion pile of orders isn't some accounting mirage cooked up by overly optimistic customers.

Artificial intelligence data centers require colossal amounts of electricity, and hyperscalers are snapping up Caterpillar's industrial gas turbines and massive reciprocating engines as fast as the company can build them. Power generation retail sales jumped 75% year over year in the third quarter, and customers are already booking production slots out to 2029 and 2030 just to get in line.

Even better, Caterpillar's momentum isn't trapped in a strict artificial intelligence silo. Construction equipment sales to end users climbed 20% in the third quarter -- marking seven straight quarters of growth -- thanks to major infrastructure projects and commercial builds. Even the mining division is seeing healthy order rates for copper and gold operations.

However, management expects 58% of the current backlog to convert into revenue within the next 12 months.

Rather than a temporary double-ordering boost, Caterpillar is backing the demand with physical capacity expansions. The company plans to nearly triple large reciprocating engine production by 2030 along with a $1 billion manufacturing investment in North Carolina.

For long-term investors, the massive backlog provides multi-year revenue visibility as the capacity constraints gradually ease.

#2887. Rockets Scurry Over Suns To Another 116-104 Victory

Another game, another 12pt victory that never felt comfortable, but never seemed much in doubt, either. While the previous opponent, the LA Clippers are not a good team, the Suns are good, or at least decent. They beat the Rockets in Phoenix not that long ago, in a game that has been described as strange, or one where the Rockets seemed asleep.

Because I've been dealing with an household electrical problem (that took out the refrigerator) which, of course, cropped up on Thanksgiving Eve; this might be a shorter recap. (I fixed the problem BTW, Thanksgiving is saved, hooray for me.) It's also a shorter recap because it's hard to pin much of a narrative on the past two Rockets game. They look like easy wins, but weren't. The Rockets didn't play their best, but won. The defense didn't seem great, but was. No one player stood out as the star, or the goat, of the team. The offense didn't look great, but it was above NBA average.

In both of the games, Clippers and Suns, it seemed as though the Rockets knew they were more talented than their opponent, turned on the afterburners for a short time, and then just played keep away.

Here's what I mean – quarter by quarter scoring tonight:

The Rockets scored a lot for the first half, then just kind of added slightly to a lead that spent the time from around the middle of the second quarter onward fluctuating between about 10 to 6 points.

The Suns obviously came out in this game with a plan to play physically with the Rockets. This lead to a whopping 26 free throws in the first half for the Rockets. In troubling news, the Rockets made only 14 of those 26 free throws, or this could have been a substantially more comfortable win. Sengun went 2-6, Thompson 4-9, Jabari 3-5, though Durant was more consistent, at 5-6. Where the good free throw shooting from the earliest part of the season has gone is anyone's guess. It hasn't cost the Rockets as it did in Denver, but neither the Suns nor the Clippers are a strong a team as the Nuggets. If it doesn't improve, though, it might cost them as soon as Saturday, when the Rockets face the 15-4 Philadelphia 76ers.

The Suns changed tack in the second half, apparently, and the Rockets only shot six more free throws in the second half.

The Rockets continue not to have a single fulcrum in terms of scoring or usage, but it's clear the engine remains Alperen Sengun. Sengun is averaging 22pts/11rbs/7ast/1stl/1blk/3TO on 55%FG shooting, 63TS%, 50%3pt on 26% usage in 33mpg. If you asked most NBA fans who was playing better, Sengun or Luka Doncic, they'd all say Doncic. But Doncic is shooting worse overall at 46%, his True Shooting Percentage is lower, despite him taking over 10FT per game, his assists are about 3/4 of an assist higher, than Sengun, his rebounds 4 lower, and his usage is sitting at a staggering 41% compared to Sengun's 26%. The argument can be made that Doncic's gaudy points total is largely driven by usage, while Sengun is the more effective player. Yes, Doncic is a heliocentric player, so perhaps he gets less help (perhaps), but that's by design.

Anyhow, Sengun has been fantastic, those are excellent numbers, and his effect is similarly profound for the Rockets. He remains, despite everything, 24 1/3 years old. He's also making 50% of his threes.

At some point we will have to have a serious conversation about Ime Udoka's truest soulmate, Marcus Smart. He's an intense player, and an incredibly streaky shooter. He also can simply lose his mind in a game, and throw a punch, or beef with a fan, or any of a variety of undesirable things. Tonight he almost single-handedly lead a Phoenix comeback tonight by committing a shooting foul on three consecutive plays before he was thankfully removed. Were they all fouls? Pretty much, yes.

We also got a waning seconds, late game stare down standoff, between Dillon Brooks and Kevin Durant. It looked for an instant like Durant was contemplating throwing a punch. If he was Marcus Smart, he probably would have done it. Instead he settled for an unnecessary dunk with seconds remaining. This marked another good game for Durant with 20pts on 7-15 shooting but a woeful 1-7 from three. Extrapolating from that he was almost perfect with his mid range shot, and it looking exactly as great as 7-8.

The next highest scorer was Amen Thompson at 18 points, and it could have been better if he made more free throws. Amen has returned to form, and he had a lot of space to work with near the rim tonight as the Suns seemed extremely focused on Sengun.

The Rockets looked like they might give this one away in the fourth quarter, but the man the Rockets missed last season, Fred VanVleet did indeed ride to the rescue, steadied the ship, toed the line, and took the wheel. So for a second game things might look a bit better on the scoreboard than the Rockets played. At least their mental fragility didn't wreck them. Dillon Brooks would not have let them hear the end of it.

The Rockets and Suns overall played a pretty feisty, chippy game with a lot of the player chosen with the Rockets pick last year Khaman Maluach trying to molest Alperen Sengun in a basketball sense. This was not successful, but he's a powerful player with a nice close shooting touch who might one day be excellent. Today was not that day.

Tomorrow, though, is Thanksgiving! On behalf of TDS, happy Thanksgiving to all who celebrate, and our best wishes to those who don't.

#2885. Bills vs. Chiefs, Week 12 preview: 5 questions with Arrowhead Pride

Five questions is back this week, Rumblers! All eyes are on the Buffalo Bills and Kansas City Chiefs when they play, which means there's always room to chat about the greatest current NFL rivalry. That it's happening on Thanksgiving night is gravy.

If you've been a Bills fan long enough, you already know the mystique and magic that surrounds this head-to-head series between quarterbacks Josh Allen and Patrick Mahomes. But there's so much more to these games than that alone.

To get us ready for Bills-Chiefs I spoke with Maurice Elston, who writes in-depth analysis, film study breakdowns, and more for Arrowhead Pride. Here, we dive a bit further into who and what the Chiefs click in 2026.

Below, Maurice and I tackle everything from the biggest differences between this year's team and previous rosters, plus a look at Kansas City's greatest strength and most glaring weakness. We also dive into what wide receiver Josh Downs has brought to the team since joining the team at the trade deadline.

Then, Maurice explains what played into the Chiefs' pair of losses compared to their dominant wins, and he closes with a look at what has to go right if Kansas City is to win at Highmark Stadium.

Read on below for some fantastic insight from Maurice into a team you can't ever learn enough about!

1. Catch us up with this season's Chiefs, if you can. What's different about them as compared to previous seasons?

Maurice Elston: The biggest difference is that the Chiefs finally have an efficient running game—and a coaching staff willing to lean into it and stick with it. Kenneth Walker III has given Kansas City the dynamic running back it has lacked for much of the Patrick Mahomes era. Now, with Walker injured, fifth-round rookie Emmett Johnson has demonstrated that the success of the running game can continue without him.

That has made the Chiefs a much more balanced offense. They can operate from under center, use play action and stay committed to the run instead of placing the entire game on Mahomes' shoulders. Mahomes is still capable of taking over when necessary, but he no longer has to perform heroics every week for Kansas City to win. That balance has been the defining difference between this team and the Chiefs offenses of recent seasons.

2. At 8-2, what do you see as the Chiefs' greatest strength, and their most glaring weakness — and how might either be impacted vs. Bills?

Maurice Elston: Kansas City's greatest strength is its balance. The Chiefs can run the football effectively and build their play-action passing game from it, which is something they haven't consistently had during the Mahomes era. Even without Walker, Johnson has rushed for 206 yards over the last two games and shown that Kansas City doesn't have to abandon its offensive identity.

The most glaring weakness is the lack of continuity along the offensive line. The Chiefs still haven't played a game with the original starting five they envisioned entering training camp. Injuries and lineup changes have forced players into unfamiliar roles throughout the season.

That will be important against Buffalo, especially with right tackle Jaylon Moore ruled out. The Bills know Kansas City will want to establish Johnson and protect Mahomes with play action. If Buffalo can stop the run without committing extra defenders and then attack the Chiefs' backup tackles, that could disrupt the balance that has made Kansas City so successful.

3. What has WR Josh Downs brought to/done for the offense since KC traded for him?

Maurice Elston: If the Chiefs were missing anything at wide receiver, it was someone who could consistently create separation against man and press coverage. Downs has brought them a polished route runner who understands how to defeat leverage, set defenders up with his releases and find openings in the middle of a defense.

The production has already been encouraging. In his first two games with Kansas City, Downs has eight receptions for 112 yards and a touchdown, with six of those catches resulting in first downs. That is significant production considering how little time he has had to learn Andy Reid's offense.

His presence also helps the other receivers because defenses now have another player they must respect opposite Travis Kelce. The more comfortable Downs becomes with the playbook, the more Kansas City can expand his role. He gives the Chiefs a separation skill set that has largely been missing since Tyreek Hill left Kansas City.

4. KC's two losses came against playoff/SB teams in the Broncos and Seahawks. What's the real story behind those defeats — especially only putting up a single field goal against Seattle — as compared to the way they've played in eight wins?

Maurice Elston: The two losses were different. Kansas City moved the ball and scored 24 points against Denver, but pressure and offensive-line breakdowns hurt the Chiefs in critical situations. Seattle was a complete offensive collapse.

The Seahawks committed to stopping Walker, challenged Kansas City's receivers in man coverage and generated pressure without having to compromise their coverage. Kansas City finished with only 213 yards, while Mahomes completed just six of 13 passes for 44 yards and an interception when pressured. When the receivers couldn't separate and the offensive line couldn't hold up in obvious passing situations, Mahomes became unsettled and the entire offense lost its rhythm.

Since those losses, Downs has given the Chiefs another answer against man coverage, Kahlil Benson has gained valuable experience at left tackle and Mahomes has looked increasingly comfortable in the pocket. Those are positive developments, but the real test begins now. Kansas City's upcoming stretch against contending teams will show whether those problems have truly been corrected or were merely hidden during the eight victories.

5. What do you see as the biggest key to success for the Chiefs, if they accomplish their main goal Thanksgiving night?

Maurice Elston: As dynamic as James Cook has been, the biggest key is containing Josh Allen and eliminating the explosive plays he creates outside the structure of Buffalo's offense.

Many of Allen's most damaging plays happen when a defense appears to have him stopped. A pass rusher loses containment, Allen escapes the pocket and suddenly turns a potential sack into a long run or a completion downfield. Kansas City's defensive line must maintain disciplined rush lanes instead of becoming so focused on sacks that it gives Allen an escape route.

When the Chiefs have an opportunity to bring him down, they must finish the play. Keeping Allen in the pocket, forcing him to operate on schedule and preventing those backbreaking second-reaction plays will be the biggest key to Kansas City leaving Buffalo with a Thanksgiving night victory.

My sincere thanks to Maurice Elston for his generous time and willingness to connect with me for five questions this week. I predict we'll get another round of questions later in winter. You can read more of Maurice's writing over at Arrowhead Pride. Be sure to check out my turn in the hot seat answering five of Maurice's questions, at AP!

#2883. Rater Confidential: Top 100 Courses in the U.S. takeaways

GOLF's Top 100 course panelists are among the most respected and well-traveled course evaluators in the game. And with the recent release of GOLF's Top 100 Courses in the U.S. ranking for 2026-27, we asked a few of them to weigh in on some of the biggest talking points.

What's your biggest takeaway from GOLF's newest Top 100 Courses in the U.S. list? Does anything stand out? Does anything surprise you?

Jon Orszag: Three things stand out. First, the remarkable stability at the top. All 25 courses in today's top 25 were there four years ago — and the top five are unchanged. Despite endless debates about individual courses, there's a strong and sustained consensus about America's very best golf architecture. Second, the impact of restorations. Some of the most exciting developments in American golf aren't new courses at all, but rather Golden Age masterpieces brought back to life. Like great architecture of any kind, great golf architecture is timeless, and sometimes the best way to improve a golf course is to rediscover what made it great in the first place. Third, the list establishes today's architectural Big Three (but really four people) — Coore & Crenshaw, Doak, and Hanse. Their original designs and restorations touch 60 of the Top 100, including 45 restorations. In terms of influence, they are this generation's Golden Age giants — today's Tillinghast, Ross, MacKenzie, and Macdonald/Raynor.

Henry Shimp: I'm always interested to see how courses move in the 51-150 bucket, as those are often the ones making changes to move into that top 50. With a course like Yale, that clearly paid off big time. However, multiple courses saw renovation dollars yield a negative rankings ROI, such as Congressional, Olympic Lake and Scioto. In the same vein, it's very interesting, and encouraging, to see courses like St Louis CC, Old Elm and Kittansett all make meaningful jumps by simply making small course improvements over time, yet no major restoration work.

Michael Blackham: Thirty years after Sand Hills kicked off the modern building boom, the old guys are still winning. Thirty-eight of the top 50 are classic-era designs. Only Sand Hills and Friar's Head crack the top 20, and Sand Hills is the only modern course in the top 10. Even one of the modern entries, the Lido, is a recreation of a 1917 Macdonald design. Some of today's new courses are terrific, but the best architecture is still mostly a century old.

The post Rater Confidential: Top 100 Courses in the U.S. takeaways appeared first on Golf.

#2881. The Buffett Indicator Is Flashing Red. These 2 Dividend Stocks Could Be Safe Havens.

The Buffett indicator is well above its long-term average, so you may want to consider buying stocks of companies that sell things people don't want to live without.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 26, 2026 at 2:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Johnson & Johnson ›

Johnson & Johnson (JNJ -0.91%) and Procter & Gamble (PG +0.44%) sell products that are bought in both good times and bad. That's notable right now because the so-called Buffett indicator is more than two standard deviations above its historical average, suggesting a high risk of a downturn. If you are a conservative investor, safe-haven stocks in the healthcare and consumer staples sectors should be high on your wish list. Here's why.

Warren Buffett was the former CEO of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +0.54%)(BRKB +0.37%). His long-term investment approach resulted in the company achieving incredible long-term returns for investors. A key piece of the story was his penchant for waiting to buy until stock prices were attractive. That meant holding off during periods of overvaluation. The tool he used to determine the valuation of the broader market was the total value of the stock market divided by gross domestic product (GDP).

It is a rough gauge, at best. But when the Buffett indicator is at an extreme level, as it is today, it's worth taking a step back. You may want to reevaluate your portfolio, perhaps holding more cash. Or, if you want to remain invested, increasing your exposure to more conservative dividend stocks that have a history of holding up during periods of economic and market weakness. That's exactly what you'll find with J&J and P&G.

One of the big reasons to like J&J right now is that it is a Dividend King, with the longest dividend streak of any healthcare company at 64 years. It has successfully navigated multiple recessions and bear markets over that span. While the company has changed over the years, it currently focuses on the pharmaceutical and medical device segments of the broader healthcare sector.

The big story is that drugs and medical devices usually aren't optional purchases. Consumers are willing to pay for them because they improve their lives and can sometimes mean the difference between life and death. That makes J&J very resilient, a fact bolstered by its diversification across two business lines.

The one problem is valuation. J&J isn't cheap right now, but for conservative investors, it may be worth paying a premium if the lofty level of the Buffett indicator has you worried. That said, the healthcare giant's 1.9% dividend yield is well above the 1% you'd collect from the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -0.25%) and the healthcare average of roughly 1.3%.

P&G is one of the world's largest consumer staples companies. It has a strong brand portfolio focused on paper products, toiletries, and makeup. Most of its products sit at the high end of their categories, which would seem like a risky place to be during an economic downturn. However, P&G has long focused on innovation, producing products that consumers are willing to pay more for. Notably, despite belt-tightening in many households today, P&G's organic sales grew 4% in the first quarter of fiscal 2027.

And consumers aren't likely to stop buying their favorite toilet paper and toothpaste even if there is a downturn. At least that's what history has shown, helping P&G to increase its dividend for 70 consecutive years. Like J&J, that's the best streak in its peer group.

P&G isn't exactly cheap, either. However, its price-to-sales and price-to-earnings ratios are roughly in line with their five-year averages. That suggests the stock is fairly valued right now. And the stock's 2.5% yield is above the consumer staples average of 2%, so you are getting a generous yield.

The Buffett indicator is flashing bright red warning signs right now, given its elevated level. If that has you thinking about dialing back on risk, you should consider reliable dividend stocks from defensive sectors like healthcare and consumer staples. The best dividend records in those two sectors belong to Johnson & Johnson and Procter & Gamble. It's probably a good idea to start your search with these two safe-haven stocks.

#2880. I'd Buy Anthropic Stock Early — but Only If I Liked the Answers to These 3 Questions

The AI lab's revenue is soaring, but the outlook for its profitability and valuation will determine whether the stock is worth buying at current prices.

By Manali Pradhan, CFA – Nov 26, 2026 at 2:17PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT +1.08%), the company behind the Claude AI ecosystem, priced its initial public offering (IPO) at $83 per share. However, when trading began on Nov. 19, the stock opened at $111.50, a 34.3% premium to that price. Shares closed at $81.90 on Nov. 24, leaving investors who bought at the opening price with a loss of over 26%.

Still, investor interest in the stock is high. Nearly seven in 10 stock investors surveyed by The Motley Fool said they planned to buy OpenAI, Anthropic, or both at their IPOs.

However, there are three specific questions that investors will want to answer before buying Anthropic stock.

Anthropic's revenue surged more than tenfold from $1.38 billion in the third quarter of 2025 to $16.96 billion in the third quarter of 2026. The number of customers generating at least $100,000 in annual revenue for Anthropic also increased from more than 950 to over 11,900 in the same time frame. The company had more than 1,900 customers that spent at least $1 million with it on an annualized basis at the end of the third quarter. This indicates that businesses are increasingly spending substantial amounts on the Claude AI ecosystem.

However, Anthropic's revenue depends heavily on customer usage. Consumption-based fees accounted for approximately 79% of revenue in the first nine months of 2026. When businesses expand their use of Claude Code and other applications, they generate more revenue for Anthropic. However, customers could also reduce spending by limiting their token use or by switching to lower-priced AI models or competing services.

Anthropic reported net revenue retention of approximately 900% at the end of September. This highlights the rapid spending growth among the company's existing customers. However, that calculation includes customers who generated as little as $1 in revenue in the prior-year quarter. Hence, the 900% figure may partly reflect rapid expansion from a low starting base rather than a growth rate Anthropic can sustain.

Pricing is another concern. Anthropic disclosed that price reductions and contractual discounts that it introduced in the second and third quarters cut its revenue by approximately $600 million.

With OpenAI's ChatGPT, Alphabet's (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%) Gemini, and other artificial intelligence (AI) competitors competing for enterprise customers, Anthropic will need to maintain strong usage growth without sacrificing too much on pricing.

Anthropic reported an operating loss of $567 million in the third quarter, compared with a $974 million profit in the second quarter. However, adjusted operating income increased from $2.24 billion to $2.46 billion. The adjusted figure excludes stock-based compensation and certain other expenses paid in shares rather than cash. Such payments do reduce other shareholders' ownership stakes in the company.

Computing expenses were a major concern. Anthropic's revenue increased sequentially by $5.4 billion in the third quarter, while computing and infrastructure expenses rose by $4.3 billion. Hence, the increase in computing costs was equivalent to nearly 80% of the additional revenue.

Adjusted operating income, however, increased by only $228 million during the same period. Hence, investors need to consider just what fraction of Anthropic's future revenue growth will drop down to the bottom line as profits.

Anthropic's adjusted operating margin declined from 19% in the second quarter to 15% in the third quarter, mainly due to $5 billion in costs associated with leasing computing capacity under expensive short-term deals. The company expects to replace some of that with capacity from cheaper long-term arrangements. However, its continued payments for computing infrastructure could put pressure on cash flow.

Anthropic has firm commitments to spend approximately $900.9 billion on leasing computing and infrastructure, $451.6 billion of which is expected to be spent by the end of 2030. These commitments extend over several years, with some subject to conditions or termination rights.

Anthropic generated $6.57 billion in operating cash flow during the first nine months of 2026. However, it also spent $2.88 billion on equipment purchases and prepayments for leased assets and equipment. Hence, investors should watch whether Anthropic can generate enough cash from operations to fund its growing infrastructure needs.

Anthropic had a market capitalization of approximately $1.1 trillion as of the close of trading on Nov. 24. Hence, the stock was trading at about 31 times its trailing-12-month revenue of $35.28 billion. The valuation multiple drops to around 13.4 times when using Anthropic's annualized revenue run rate of $81 billion at the end of October. However, the run rate reflects recent customer spending rather than revenues earned over a full year.

Both metrics show investors are already paying a substantial premium for Anthropic's expected future growth.

Another concern for investors who buy now is the looming increase in Anthropic’s float — the volume of its shares available for trading. Stakeholders who owned Anthropic shares prior to the IPO are subject to restrictions on selling their shares. But those lock-up periods will expire in a series of tranches from December 2026 through 2027. Each expiration date will allow those early investors to sell a larger fraction of the shares they own. This certainly does not mean these shareholders will sell quickly, but the additional availability of shares to be purchased could put downward pressure on the stock price. Future secondary share issuances could also reduce existing shareholders' ownership stakes in Anthropic.

With all that in mind, I would suggest that you buy Anthropic only if you calculate that its valuation offers sufficient upside potential, despite moderating revenue growth and potential stock dilution.

#2879. Anthropic Stock Is Now Trading as ANT. Should You Buy Today or Wait?

Tech stock IPOs have a long history of disappointing early investors.

By Matthew Benjamin – Nov 26, 2026 at 2:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT +1.08%), the company behind the popular AI chatbot Claude, began trading last week under the ticker symbol ANT following the largest initial public offering (IPO) in history.

After a strong first day, when investors bid the new stock up from its IPO price of $83 to about $111 per share, it has been falling since. Three trading days after its debut, the stock has dropped below its IPO price and traded at around $78 per share.

So, does that make this exciting new stock a buy, given that it's cheaper now?

I would say no. Even investors determined to add this stock to their portfolio should wait. That's because the initial pop in the share price, followed by a downward trend, is nothing new. In fact, IPO stocks have a long history of disappointing early investors.

IPO stocks are exciting, and that excitement often gives their shares an initial market boost. The share price of an IPO stock typically sees a first-day return of 18%, according to research by Edward Jones. Technology stocks do even better on day one, with a 35.2% increase.

But IPO tech stocks then fall, on average, 3% after three months and 14% after six months, according to that same research. So waiting six months, until the stock finds its footing beyond the initial IPO excitement, is your best bet.

Overall, it was hard for the Spurs to find an offensive rhythm outside of forcing the ball to Wembanyama in the high or low post. San Antonio shot just 43% from the field and 28% from three.

With the loss, the Spurs fall to 2-1 in the Western Conference Group C. They are in a four-way tie for first with Portland, the Golden State Warriors, and the Los Angeles Lakers. The Spurs play the Lakers, and the Blazers play the Warriors on Friday. San Antonio needs to win that game, and Portland to lose, to win the group outright. San Antonio may have a shot at the wild card spot if the Blazers and Spurs win.

Executing on store presentation and inventory alignment remains the core priority.

#2872. If a Market Correction Is Around the Corner, I'm Making This 1 Easy Move

Don't be scared of corrections. Take advantage of them.

By David Dierking – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

Anyone waiting to buy stocks at better prices got a lesson in just how quickly an opportunity can disappear.

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) CEO Jensen Huang's departure sent some U.S. stock prices sharply lower last week. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%) entered correction territory, and the Nasdaq-100 briefly sank into a bear market. By Monday morning, Huang was reinstated, and the Nasdaq-100 rebounded by nearly 7%.

How should investors handle this volatility, and what portfolio moves, if any, should you make right now?

This week's rebound was astonishingly quick in that a lot of ground was recovered in a single day. But it does follow a recent track record of rapid recoveries.

The Q1 2025 correction that culminated with President Trump's "Liberation Day" tariffs experienced a similar turnaround. After falling 19% by early April, the S&P 500 was back to all-time highs by the end of June.

Of course, there was the COVID pandemic bear market. The S&P 500 fell by more than 30% in one month's time. After a multitrillion-dollar stimulus package, the index was back to a new all-time high by August, just five months later.

It's rare to find just the right moment to buy the dip as it's happening. That's why I usually suggest keeping your systematic investment plans going as scheduled.

Purchasing shares on a set schedule is an ideal way to make at least some purchases at lower prices without trying to call a market bottom. In sharp, single-day rebounds like Monday, it may not work unless you place a trade manually ahead of time. But during corrections where the recovery process takes months, even a couple of buys along the way can reduce your cost basis significantly.

Keeping your automatic investment plans going throughout the current pullback might be especially important this time around.

A CBOE study last year found that sudden volatility shocks in the markets can result in elevated volatility that lasts for months. Volatility, of course, tends to be closely associated with falling stock prices. If that pattern follows the current correction, it could be a fair amount of time before the S&P 500 and other major equity indexes hit new highs.

But for regular investors, that can be an opportunity. The longer stocks hover below highs, the better your chance to pick up more shares at discounted prices. It may feel counterintuitive as it's happening. But continuing to make systematic monthly investments can pay off significantly in the years ahead.

Could it be a three-game win streak for the Warriors? Maybe it's enough that the team is all together on a holiday. #2866. Here’s How Many Shares of Procter & Gamble You’d Need to Own to Collect $1,000 in Annual Dividends

Shoppers are watching every dollar they spend, yet Procter & Gamble keeps growing its revenues and has raised its dividend for 70 straight years.

By John Ballard – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Procter & Gamble ›

Procter & Gamble (PG +0.44%), the company behind brands like Tide, Pampers, Gillette, Charmin, and Crest, pays a quarterly dividend of $1.0885 per share. That works out to roughly $4.35 per share each year. To collect $1,000 a year, you would need about 230 shares, which would cost just over $39,800 at the stock's Nov. 25 closing price of $173.23. That's income from household products that people use every day.

In this difficult economy, shoppers are watching every dollar they spend, yet Procter & Gamble is still growing. Organic sales (a metric that excludes the impacts of foreign currency swings and recent acquisitions) rose 4% year over year last quarter, mostly because people bought more of its products.

Management maintained its full-year outlook, even as it sees rising costs for materials, energy, and shipping. That matters because people keep buying laundry detergent, diapers, and toothpaste even when they cut back elsewhere. P&G has also raised its dividend for 70 consecutive years, making it one of the rare Dividend Kings — companies that have raised their payouts for 50 straight years or more.

The main risk P&G faces now is cost pressure. If higher costs persist, the company's profits could flatten out, leaving less room for future dividend increases. P&G can offset some of this pressure through its scale, which gives it bargaining power with suppliers and retailers, and through its cost-cutting programs.

The stock trades at about 25 times this fiscal year's earnings estimates, and analysts expect earnings to grow about 4.7% annually. That's not a bargain valuation, but it's a reasonable price for a business that keeps growing and returning a growing cash payout to shareholders year after year. For investors, P&G remains a solid stock to hold for long-term dividend income.

#2861. SpaceX Is a Great Company. Is It a Great Stock at This Price?

SpaceX's current stock price leaves no room for error.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:36PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Warren Buffett, perhaps the greatest investor of all time, once said: "It's far better to buy a wonderful company at a fair price than a fair company at a wonderful price." However, even a wonderful company isn't worth buying at just any price. Earning outstanding long-term returns requires investors to buy stocks at the right price. On that note, let's consider one popular company on the market right now: Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.30%). The company arguably boasts a very strong business, but is its current stock price fair?

SpaceX is a leader in rocket technology. It helped pioneer reusable rockets and became the leader in space launch, contracting a significant amount of business from various U.S. government agencies. The company has also become a leader in satellite-based connectivity. Its constellation of Low Earth Orbit (LEO) satellites is far larger than that of any competitor.

In addition, SpaceX offers various artificial intelligence (AI) services, including access to its Grok large language model and compute capacity through a network of data centers. It isn't just the breadth of SpaceX's business that makes it a great company. It has arguably built a robust competitive advantage. Consider the company's space launch business.

Building rockets is capital-intensive, but that's just step one. They then have to go through multiple stages of testing before they can fly regularly. That's not to mention the expensive and time-consuming process of obtaining the necessary regulatory approvals that allow companies to legally fly rockets. SpaceX has already gone through all that with its Falcon 9, a partially reusable vehicle that has helped significantly slash space launch costs.

It has also made tremendous progress with its next-gen rocket, Starship, which has now undergone more than a dozen flight tests. SpaceX's rocket technology is superior to that of its competitors. That's why it is the market leader by far. Given the financial and regulatory barriers to entry, not to mention the time it takes to develop and build rockets, the company has a strong competitive edge. There is something similar going on with SpaceX's connectivity business.

Launching satellites into orbit takes time, a lot of money, and the appropriate authorizations. Also, service quality generally improves as the number of satellites increases. That's why it won't be easy for competitors to catch up to SpaceX here. Further, the company's financial results have been improving. In the third quarter, SpaceX recorded $14.6 billion in revenue, up 161% year over year.

The company's Super Intelligence segment -- through which it offers various AI services -- posted $7.6 billion in revenue, up 830% year over year. Connectivity revenue was $5.2 billion, 69.5% higher than the year-ago period.

And space revenue was $1.8 billion, up 6% year over year. On the bottom line, SpaceX recorded a net income of $4.4 billion, versus a net loss of $1.1 billion in the prior-year quarter. SpaceX's improving financials, strong lead and competitive moat across two of its business segments, and fast-growing AI business make it a robust company.

Now for the second question: Is SpaceX attractive at its current price? Shares are trading at about $126 as of writing, and its market cap is $1.7 trillion. Based on its trailing 12-month revenue of $32.04 billion, the stock is trading at about 53x sales. That's very high, but the company deserves a premium, given the strength of the business and how fast it is growing.

Also, it's perhaps more telling to look at the forward-looking earnings multiple. SpaceX's current forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, based on expected earnings over the next 12 months, is 44.2 -- that, again, looks high, and we have to factor in some uncertainty with the company's prospects. SpaceX is significantly exposed to regulatory risks across all three of its businesses.

Also, its fastest-growing segment, SI, which now accounts for more than half of its top line, is the unit where its moat is arguably less established. The SI business also risks a significant slowdown, which would reduce year-over-year revenue growth and margins, given how much SpaceX is spending on its AI build-out.

With all that said, I'd consider buying SpaceX at roughly 30x forward earnings -- that may undermine its prospects somewhat, but it would also give me a relatively healthy margin of error. But that means that at its current 44.2x forward earnings, the stock is too expensive, at least for me. I'd wait for a steeper decline to initiate a position.

#2857. Sandisk’s 8-for-1 Split Is Done. Here’s What Investors Should Do Next.

Sandisk's stock split is finished, so the only question left is whether its AI-fueled flash memory business can hold up as prices cool.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 26, 2026 at 4:00PM EST

When a stock splits, the headlines tend to make it sound like something big just happened, but for Sandisk (SNDK -5.57%) shareholders, the real decision starts now that the paperwork is done and the share price looks a lot friendlier.

Sandisk announced its 8-for-1 forward split alongside its fiscal first-quarter results on Oct. 29, giving every holder of record on Nov. 9 seven extra shares for each one they owned. The change became effective on Nov. 19, and split-adjusted trading began the next morning.

A stock split, which is when a company divides each existing share into several cheaper ones, works like cutting a pizza into more slices without adding any cheese. Sandisk itself said that its overall market capitalization and stockholder ownership percentages would not be affected, and it framed the move as a way to make shares more accessible, including to employees buying through its stock purchase plan.

What the split can't do is change the business, so that's where investors should focus.

Sandisk makes NAND flash, which is the memory chip that keeps your data when the power goes off, and AI has turned that once-sleepy product into a hot commodity.

In the quarter that ended Oct. 2, revenue jumped 378% year over year to $11 billion, data center sales rose 61% from the prior quarter, and gross margin, which is the share of each sales dollar left after the cost of making the product, reached 85%.

CEO David Goeckeler said in the release, "We have started fiscal year 2027 with another quarter of strong performance, with robust and growing demand for our products driving outpaced growth in our data center business."

The AI connection is more specific than it sounds, because AI models need fast storage to hold the context of a conversation. On the earnings call, Goeckeler said, "We believe this is a secular dynamic that is expanding NAND's role across the AI stack."

The part I find most reassuring is Sandisk's long-term customer deals, which it calls New Business Models or NBMs. About half of its fiscal 2027 supply and two-thirds of its fiscal 2028 supply are already committed, 95% of those deals are with investment-grade companies, and Goeckeler said the contracts have "become the predominant way we deal with our customers." CFO Luis Visoso went further on the call, saying, "Our NBM pipeline exceeds our supply throughout the end of the decade."

Those contracts matter because flash prices are cooling. Research firm TrendForce now expects NAND contract prices to fall as much as 5% in the first quarter of 2027, with declines spreading across all product categories after that. Concentration adds risk too, since two customers each made up 12% of revenue last quarter.

The stock has already priced in plenty of that worry. Shares closed at $87.71 on Nov. 23, roughly 70% below their split-adjusted 52-week high, and trade at a price-to-earnings ratio of about 5.6. Management guided for split-adjusted earnings of $6.88 to $7.13 per share in the current quarter, which puts the stock near 3 times annualized earnings.

Visoso also cautioned on the call that the March quarter "is normally our weakest sequential quarter," so results may look less dazzling by spring, but the company still bought back $5 billion of stock last quarter, and Visoso said, "Our repurchases reflect our conviction in the long-term value of the business and our disciplined approach to capital allocation."

I think long-term investors who can stomach big swings should consider a modest position in Sandisk, sized for a flash market that could get rougher before it gets better, and add only if the long-term contracts keep holding up as prices fall. The split changed the price tag, but the contracts will decide the value.

A native of Kenya, West Virginia, first attended Virginia Tech for his first two collegiate seasons before transferring to WVU. After transferring to West Virginia, he stated every game in 2021 and 2022 at right guard, before moving to right tackle for his redshirt senior season in 2023. Steelers guard Doug Nester during an OTAs practice on May 29, 2025. — Ed Thompson / Steelers Now

#2851. There’s something to like about the way the Suns lost in Houston

The Phoenix Suns went to Space City to battle the team whose logo resembles one of those silly little patches that go on astronaut suits, ultimately losing by 12. What that final score doesn't tell you is that with 4:45 remaining, the Suns were down three points. What killed them down the stretch is what killed them throughout the game, and it shouldn't come as a surprise to any of us. Rebounding was the culprit. Or, more specifically, their inability to rebound.

Phoenix was outrebounded 51-38 overall and 20-13 on the offensive glass, but it was the 27 second-chance points they surrendered that ultimately sunk them. When Alperen Sengun pulls down 11 rebounds, and seven of them are of the offensive variety, you know you've done something wrong.

But my takeaway from this game? The impressively physical brand of basketball the Suns played. They stood up to a Rockets team that clearly had revenge on its mind as they ran up and down that bright red clowny court. After all, Phoenix had already beaten Houston earlier this season. When the Rockets came out with a chip on their shoulder, the Suns rose to the challenge and battled them throughout.

The final result comes down to two things. Houston possesses a more talented roster, and they're bigger, badder, and better on the boards. The attitude with which the Suns played, however, was something I thoroughly enjoyed watching. That's who this team was a season ago, and perhaps they needed a team like Houston to push them around a little, to jostle them enough to draw a line in the sand and say, "We're done with this."

The hope is that they carry that attitude forward. Because if they can bring that level of intensity and fight night in and night out, nobody is going to want to play that version of the Phoenix Suns. We know that because we saw it last year.

The rebounding issues aren't going away, and there will be nights when Phoenix simply doesn't possess the personnel to overcome them. But physicality and effort are things this team can control regardless of the opponent. What we witnessed in Houston was a willingness to compete on somebody else's terms without being intimidated by them. The Suns lost the basketball game, but somewhere on that obnoxiously red court, they rediscovered a little bit of their swagger.

Jalen Green did Jalen Green things against the Pelicans, logging his third Baller of the season with his 25-point performance on Monday.

#2834. Grading the Mavericks: Dallas’ accountability helped prevent a serious fall

The Mavericks went 4-2 over the last two weeks and sit at 11-7, in eighth place in the West. They lost back-to-back road games to Cleveland (127-117) and Boston (131-94), but rebounded with four straight wins against Golden State (116-94), the Clippers (110-100), Utah (116-114), and Denver (120-101). Cooper Flagg led the team in scoring with 23.7 points per game.

The Mavericks did not start the last two weeks well. Losing back-to-back road games in embarrassing fashion, then giving a suboptimal performance against a really hurt Golden State team was pretty dire, despite the large margin of victory. The three games since have been pretty convincing; the Mavericks are a good team somewhere in the realm of "better than bad, not elite, but can win on any night".

We have seen the emergence, or more accurately, the rebirth of Caleb Martin in the last six games. He is scoring over 10 points per game, shooting 56.5 percent from three, and grabbing 2.3 steals per game over that stretch. Martin is the true definition of a glue guy and has held the team together in their four-game win streak.

The win against Denver was incredible. It was an utterly dominant defensive performance. It is the type of win you have to build off of and refuse to let the train slow down. It was also Dallas' third NBA Cup victory, meaning if they beat Phoenix on Friday, they will win their group.

Dallas' two losses that applied to this round's grades were part of a three-game skid that lacked effort in every sense of the word. Losing to a Cleveland team that sat all of their starters after travel issues was bad, but going into Boston the next day and losing the game in roughly six minutes (after Dallas went down 24-4) was a true wake-up call. Cooper Flagg said after the loss: "I don't think we competed at a high enough level, you know, throughout the three games".

Every viewer noticed that, but having a guy in the fire acknowledge their shortcomings showed they could get through the adversity. And they did, to the tune of four straight wins. Even after the third victory in Utah, where they squandered a 17-point lead in three-and-a-half minutes, Kyrie Irving held the team, including the coaches, after practice to run in preparation for their game in Denver's altitude. It paid off with a 19-point road victory.

That is the sign of a good team. Being able to have honest conversations about your play and notice when things need to change is a key component of success. The Mavericks clearly have that. There will be more adversity and plenty more losses to come this season. If the Mavericks can lean on this framework, they will be solid, no matter what happens.

The Mavericks already have a weird roster, but the player that sticks out the most is Naji Marshall. He is not a good enough defender to justify the offensive dominance, and he turns the ball over far too much to be handling the ball at the rate he does. Part of his 20 percent usage rate is the fact that the roster is lacking ball handlers, but some of it, and perhaps the most frustrating part, is that he simply chooses to play hero ball far too often.

It's not clear when you look at his 10.9 points in 24.1 minutes per game. And sometimes he does play well enough to matter. But it's the choice to keep a dribble handoff, or the pass that he forces in transition, or the insistence on taking floaters and shrinking the floor that really hurts the team. The extension Dallas signed him to already feels cumbersome, and I expect the best version of this team to not have him on the floor. Whether that is because he is benched or traded away, I am not sure.

Maybe we have been spoiled by the likes of Luka Doncic and Cooper Flagg, or it's the guys picked behind him playing well, but Johnson Jr., the ninth overall pick in this past draft, has felt overwhelmingly underwhelming. His athleticism does not jump off the screen a lot of the time, his offensive awareness is sketchy, and he fouls a bit too much to stay in the game consistently. That being said, he has shown flashes of being a really good glue piece to a great defensive team.

One of these came against Dallas' statement win in Denver, where he scored nine points and had a team-high 10 rebounds. He played excellent defense, had a monster dunk, moved his feet incredibly well, and impacted winning on all fronts. It is this type of game that makes you wonder why he can't do this all the time. Some of it may be physical, with the shin splints still affecting him. A lot of it is probably mental. But either way, although the consistency isn't there yet, you can see the vision finally.

#2829. Lions’ Jahmyr Gibbs surpasses Barry Sanders in NFL history

Lions' Jahmyr Gibbs surpasses Barry Sanders in NFL history appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

On Thursday afternoon, the Detroit Lions hosted the Chicago Bears for a key Thanksgiving Day battle between two NFC titans. The first place spot in the NFC North was on the line in this one, as both the Lions and Bears entered this one at 8-2.

It didn't take long for Bears star running back Jahmyr Gibbs to make an imprint on this game, scoring a touchdown early in the first quarter to put his team up 10-0. In the process, he made some NFL history.

"Jahmyr Gibbs passed Barry Sanders for the most rushing TDs before turning 25," reported SportsCenter on X, formerly Twitter.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

The score was Gibbs' 53rd touchdown of his career, passing Lions legend Barry Sanders' mark of 52. Gibbs won't be 25 years old until next season starts, so he will likely extend his new record significantly before this year ends.

Meanwhile, the touchdown was also Gibbs' first score on Thanksgiving of his career.

The Bears and Lions both headed into this game riding significant momentum, and Lions wide receiver Amon-Ra St. Brown provided some fireworks early by hitting the Stanky Leg dance after making a big catch, appearing to mock Bears head coach and former Detroit offensive coordinator Ben Johnson in the process.

Gibbs has been a huge part of the Lions' offensive attack for multiple years now, and the pressure will be even higher on the Detroit offense to put up big numbers each week now that Aidan Hutchinson is out for the year.

After this game, next up for the Lions is a road tilt vs the Atlanta Falcons next week.

#2819. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2817. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2816. Open Thread: Giving thanks for Carter Bryant

Today carter Bryant turns 21. Now in his second year, Bryant is finding his place on a Spurs team determined to return to the Finals. He's developing his three-point shot, taking more catch-and-shoot opportunities while on the floor.

While emerging as a three-point threat, he continues to play aggressive defense, often taking on some high-profile physical opponents. In addition to his 3-and-D growth, CB displays brutal slam dunk abilities.

This is a volatile era for Bryant as he seeks a larger role on the team. To make space and add minutes, Carter begun coming off the bench before Harrison Barnes and on occasion even Keldon Johnson.

This year, Bryant celebrates his birthday on Thanksgiving. Nothing like having a holiday overshadow your birthday. But like everything else involving Carter Bryant, he's quietly raising his profile without the need for a spotlight.

Happy birthday, Carter. Eat, drink (legally), and be merry, for tomorrow you take on the Los Angeles Lakers, one of the Spurs oldest rivals and the only team whose record mirrors the Spurs. There is a trip to the NBA Cup elimination rounds on the line, and this is now a must-win game if the Spurs want to keep their Cup dreams alive.

Chicago Bears' Colston Loveland (84) is carted off the field after an injury against the Detroit Lions in Detroit, Thursday, Nov. 26, 2026. AP Photo/Paul Sancya

#2811. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.
